# Research Sources Log
### OT/ICS Deception Placement Optimization — M.Tech Thesis

Running catalog of every source surfaced across research iterations. **Appended after each iteration — never rewritten from scratch.** Use this to pull citations for the thesis lit review; verify each before final citation, since some links below are secondary aggregators rather than the primary publisher.

**Legend:** 🟢 verified · 🟡 named but unconfirmed · 🔴 needs a different access route (`00-workflow-and-rules.md`, rule 3). *(D34: this line used to define only 🟢 "link captured" and 🔴 "link not captured". Entries written before rule 3 use those older meanings.)*

## Iteration 1 Sources

### A. Open-source / academic OT-ICS honeypot tools
1. **Conpot** — MushMush Foundation. ICS honeypot emulating Modbus, S7comm, SNMP, BACnet, IEC 60870-5-104. The field's de-facto baseline. 🟢 https://github.com/mushorg/conpot
2. **HoneyPLC** — López-Morales, Rubio-Medrano, Doupé, Shoshitaishvili, Wang, Bao, Ahn (2020), ACM CCS. High/medium-interaction PLC honeypot with a Profiler tool; profiles Siemens S7-300/1200/1500, Allen-Bradley MicroLogix 1100, ABB PM554; fools Nmap/Shodan Honeyscore. 🔴
3. **ICSLure** — Furfaro et al. (2025), arXiv:2509.04080. Very-high-interaction honeynet integrating real PLCs + physics-aware plant simulation, deployed via Ansible. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04080
4. **HoneyICS** — (2023), ACM ARES. High-interaction, physics-aware PLC+HMI honeynet. 🔴
5. **GasPot** — Veeder-Root Guardian AST tank-gauge simulator (oil & gas sector). 🔴
6. **GridPot** — electric-grid honeynet. 🔴
7. **CryPLH** — Buza, Juhász, Miru, Félegyházi, Holczer (2014), SmartGridSec. Siemens S7-300 smart-grid PLC honeypot (HTTP/HTTPS/SNMP/S7); noted to fail against Nmap fingerprinting. 🔴
8. **SCADA HoneyNet Project** — Cisco/Honeynet Project. Early convincing SCADA deception effort. 🔴
9. **LLMPot** — LLM-based automated ICS protocol-emulation honeypot (2024–2025). 🔴
10. **Hybrid Honeypot (Purdue-model framed)** — CEUR-WS Vol-4198. Frames ICS honeypot design explicitly within Purdue Model levels. 🔴

### B. OT-specific deception research systems
11. **DecIED** — Yang, Cheh, Chen, Mashima et al. (2020), ACM CPSS Workshop. IEC 61850-compliant smart-substation deception; k-anonymous decoy IEDs (>200 on one industrial PC); GOOSE/SV-synchronized. 🟢 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3384941.3409592
12. **Mashima (2022)** — "MITRE ATT&CK Based Evaluation on In-Network Deception Technology for Modernized Electrical Substation Systems," *Sustainability* 14(3):1256. Evaluates DecIED against ATT&CK-ICS and Stuxnet-class attacks. 🟢 https://www.mdpi.com/2071-1050/14/3/1256 (also 🟡 ResearchGate copy: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/358063236_MITRE_ATTCK_Based_Evaluation_on_In-Network_Deception_Technology_for_Modernized_Electrical_Substation_Systems)
13. **Decepti-SCADA** — Cifranic, Hallman, Romero-Mariona, Souza, Calton, Coca (2020), *Internet of Things* 12:100320 (ScienceDirect); earlier IoTBDS 2020 conference version on Scitepress. SCADA-specific decoy generation/obfuscation for critical infrastructure; manual GUI-driven placement. 🟢 https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S2542660520301517 · 🟢 https://www.scitepress.org/Papers/2020/93433/93433.pdf · 🟡 Semantic Scholar: https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Decepti-SCADA:-A-cyber-deception-framework-for-of-Cifranic-Hallman/87e608da45132eea4654a875b31113dd95085bec
14. **"Beyond the honeypot: How OT deception is reshaping active defense in ICS networks"** — Industrial Cyber (trade article; interviews CounterCraft, Fortinet, Fidelis Security execs). 🟢 https://industrialcyber.co/features/beyond-the-honeypot-how-ot-deception-is-reshaping-active-defense-in-ics-networks/

### D. Placement-optimization literature (enterprise IT / Active Directory / cloud) — closest methodological precedents
24. **Zambianco, Facchinetti, Siracusa** — "A Proactive Decoy Selection Scheme for Cyber Deception using MITRE ATT&CK," *Computers & Security* 148:104144 (2025); arXiv:2404.12783. Graph-partition ILP over ATT&CK-derived attack paths, minimizing decoy count while covering attacker techniques. **⭐ Closest precedent — must be engaged directly in section 14.** 🟢 https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.12783 · https://arxiv.org/html/2404.12783 · https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0167404824004498
25. **Zambianco, Facchinetti, Doriguzzi-Corin, Siracusa** — "Resource-aware Cyber Deception for Microservice-based Applications," *IEEE Trans. Services Computing* 17(6):4211–4224 (2024); arXiv:2303.03151. Integer non-linear optimization for decoy placement under a CPU/RAM budget on a CVSS-weighted microservice attack graph; betweenness-centrality heuristic for scale. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.03151 · https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2024ITSCo..17.4211Z/abstract
26. **Ngo, Guo, Nguyen** — "Near Optimal Strategies for Honeypots Placement in Dynamic and Large Active Directory Networks," AAMAS 2023 (extended abstract). Proves NP-hardness; tree-decomposition + MIP. 🟢 https://www.ifaamas.org/Proceedings/aamas2023/pdfs/p2517.pdf
27. **Ngo, Guo, Nguyen** — "Catch Me if You Can: Effective Honeypot Placement in Dynamic AD Attack Graphs," arXiv:2312.16820. dyMIP(m) for dynamic graphs; evaluated on AD graphs up to ~12,001 nodes. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.16820 · https://arxiv.org/pdf/2312.16820
28. **Nguemkam, Anwar, Kengne Tchendji, Tosh, Kamhoua** — "Optimal Honeypot Allocation Using Core Attack Graph in Cyber Deception Games," IEEE PIMRC 2023. "Core attack graph" abstraction concentrating honeypots on critical-path nodes. 🟢 https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10293814/

    **Update, 6 October 2026.** Citation confirmed from the reference list of #29 (its ref. [16]): A. L. Nguemkam, A. H. Anwar, V. K. Tchendji, D. K. Tosh and C. Kamhoua, Proc. IEEE 34th PIMRC, Toronto, September 2023, pp. 1–6, doi:10.1109/PIMRC56721.2023.10293814. The paper itself has not been read.
29. **Nguemkam et al.** — "Optimal Honeypot Allocation Using Core Attack Graph in Partially Observable Stochastic Games," *IEEE Access* 12:187444–187455 (2024), DOI 10.1109/ACCESS.2024.3513461. Z-POSG solved via HSVI on core attack graph; scales to ~300 nodes. 🟡 https://www.researchgate.net/publication/386589022_Optimal_Honeypot_Allocation_Using_Core_Attack_Graph_in_Partially_Observable_Stochastic_Games

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢 full text read** (PDF supplied by you). Authors in full: Achile Leonel Nguemkam, Ahmed Hemida Anwar, Vianney Kengne Tchendji, Deepak K. Tosh, Charles Kamhoua; published 9 December 2024. Honeypot allocation as a partially observable stochastic game on a core attack graph, evaluated on networks of up to 300 nodes. The text never mentions industrial control, SCADA, IEC 62443 or the Purdue model: a member of the game-theoretic cluster, not an OT placement competitor.
30. **"Adaptive Honeypot Allocation in Multi-Attacker Networks via Bayesian Stackelberg Games"** — *Computers & Security* (2026); arXiv:2505.16043. MILP over a "Purdue-model-inspired layered structure" (generic graph, not real OT assets); Bayesian belief updates on attacker type. **⭐ Nearest "OT-flavored" precedent — read closely, distinguish carefully.** 🟢 https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16043
31. **Anwar, Kamhoua, Leslie** (2020) — "Honeypot Allocation over Attack Graphs in Cyber Deception Games," IEEE ICNC + Wiley book chapter. MILP for strong attacker, greedy heuristic for naive attacker. 🟡 https://www.researchgate.net/publication/340304020_Honeypot_Allocation_over_Attack_Graphs_in_Cyber_Deception_Games
32. **Milani et al.** — Stackelberg attack-graph deception game; three deceptive actions modify the attack graph; proves NP-hard; MILP solution. 🔴 (found via arXiv:2310.10667 survey citation)
33. **Bilinski, Gabrys, Mauger** (2018) — "Optimal Placement of Honeypots for Network Defense," GameSec 2018. 🔴
34. **"Optimizing Honeypot Placement Strategies with Graph Neural Networks for Enhanced Resilience via Cyber Deception"** — 2nd Graph Neural Networking Workshop 2023, ACM. GNN-based placement to avoid game-theoretic "curse of dimensionality." 🟢 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3630049.3630169

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢 full text read** (PDF supplied by you; full record at #323). A graph neural network with game-theoretic edge features replaces classical game-theoretic solution of the placement, reaching 92.34% test accuracy and a 139× inference speedup over classical methods. The scenario is restricted to one honeypot on generic networks: no OT setting and no multi-decoy budget, so not a competitor. Closes A9's first half.
35. **Hawkeyes** — hierarchical multi-agent RL honeypot placement (high-level policy by intrusion stage + asset criticality, low-level regional agents), *Computer Networks* (2025). 🔴 — possibly related: "Real-Time Defensive Strategy Selection via Deep Reinforcement Learning," ACM ARES 2023: https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3600160.3600176 (verify link matches)
36. PPO-based multi-type deception deployment — *Scientific Reports* (2025). 🔴
37. Tactical-network dynamic game deception allocation — arXiv:2308.11817. 🔴
38. "Game analysis and decision making optimization of evolutionary dynamic honeypot" — *Computers & Electrical Engineering*, ScienceDirect (2024). Evolutionary service-switching strategy vs. random allocation. 🟢 https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0045790624004610
39. Shapley Value to Rank Vulnerabilities on Attack Graphs — GameSec 2023. Outperforms plain centrality for honeypot-allocation ranking. 🔴

### E. Surveys / critique papers
40. **"Decoys Cannot Go Everywhere: Mapping the Deception Surface in MITRE ATT&CK"** — Valeros et al. (2026), arXiv:2606.27966. Four-criterion rubric across ATT&CK v18.1 (250 techniques); only 32% admit a plausible decoy. **⭐ Central to defining "candidate deception locations" rigorously.** 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.27966 **Full text read twice (30 September and 1 October 2026):** the arXiv PDF, then a 39-page printout of the arXiv HTML supplied by the user (printed 1 October), OCR'd and checked page by page against Figure 2 and Tables 1–4. arXiv:2606.27966v1 [cs.CR], 26 June 2026. Authors: Veronica Valeros and Viliam Lisý (Czech Technical University in Prague), Carlos Catania (Universidad Nacional de Cuyo), Harm Griffioen (TU Delft). **Licence: CC BY-NC-SA 4.0** (#368), stated on the arXiv page. Scope: enterprise ATT&CK v18.1 only; the ICS matrix is not scored.
    - *Scoring rules:* four-point forced-choice scale with no neutral midpoint (Yes / Mostly Yes / Mostly No / No), chosen to avoid "a default maybe or it depends". Criteria are assessed in sequence and "the first No ends the evaluation". "Where evidence fits two adjacent levels equally, we assign the lower one (closer to No)." Each criterion reflects "the best case a well-instrumented decoy could plausibly produce", and the rubric scores "what is possible in principle, not whether one specific deployment would succeed".
    - *Results relied on:* "admits a decoy" means Feasibility ≠ No (58 Yes / 12 Mostly Yes / 10 Mostly No of 80). No admitted technique scored No on any later criterion: Interaction 34/24/22/0, Intelligence Yield 71/8/1/0, Malice Fidelity 30/44/6/0 (the last No count by arithmetic, because the printout truncates that column). Discovery admits 22 decoys but only 2 score Yes on Malice Fidelity, "because legitimate users also enumerate".
    - *Reliability:* a single primary scorer, named as the main limitation. Expert study with 9 raters × 5 techniques × 4 criteria: exact agreement 0.28–0.44, ordered agreement 0.67–0.76, Krippendorff's ordinal α 0.36 / −0.06 / 0.08 / 0.12 (Feasibility / Interaction / Intelligence Yield / Malice Fidelity). The authors read this as "usable, but not settled". Interaction was hardest: experts disagreed on "whether the attacker path would realistically lead to it".
    - *Guidance:* LLM-based judges "should support rather than replace expert review". Claude Code was used as note-taker during scoring, "not as scorer". Proposed refinements include making Malice Fidelity "more explicit about benign administrative activity". An asset-centred assessment is named as future work.
    - *Framing:* the introduction criticises placement-optimisation work (game theory, attack graphs, Zambianco #24) for assuming decoys can be placed anywhere. Per-technique scores are published at a temporary URL (#370).
41. Beltrán-López, Gil Pérez, Nespoli (2026) — "Cyber Deception: Taxonomy, State of the Art, Frameworks, Trends, and Open Challenges," *IEEE Communications Surveys & Tutorials* 28:1520–1556; arXiv:2409.07194. 🔴

45. "A comprehensive survey on cyber deception techniques to improve honeypot performance" — ScienceDirect (2024). 🟢 https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0167404824000932
46. "A Survey on Industrial Control System Digital Forensics: Challenges, Advances and Future Directions" — *IEEE Communications Surveys & Tutorials*. 🟢 https://dl.acm.org/doi/abs/10.1109/COMST.2023.3264680
47. "Cybersecurity Opportunities and Risks of Artificial Intelligence in Industrial Control Systems: A Survey" — ScienceDirect. 🟢 https://www.sciencedirect.com/org/science/article/pii/S1526149226000809
48. "Cybersecurity of Industrial Cyber-Physical Systems: A Review" — *ACM Computing Surveys*. 🟢 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3510410
49. "Cybersecurity for industrial control systems: A survey" — ScienceDirect. 🟢 https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0167404819302172

## Iteration 2 Sources

### Primary sources read in full (verified, not just abstract/snippet)
190. **Zambianco, Facchinetti, Siracusa (2025)** — full text read via arXiv HTML. Confirms exact method (Multi-Terminal Vertex Separator ILP, solved with SCIP), exact eval setup (100 random instances, 1–9 attack targets, 266 ATT&CK Enterprise techniques, 153 eligible outcome-targets), and exact results (runtime 0.042–0.060s; 4 decoys optimal vs. 40 decoys for "Group" baseline at 1 target). 🟢 https://arxiv.org/html/2404.12783
191. **Zambianco, Facchinetti, Doriguzzi-Corin, Siracusa (2024)** — full text read via arXiv PDF. Confirms exact method (non-linear integer program + greedy heuristic via betweenness centrality), exact eval setup (M=100–500 microservices, N up to 20 nodes, Barabási–Albert graphs, Alibaba cloud trace dataset, Apple M1 Pro 14-core/16GB, 100 configs, 95% CI), exact results (decoy-to-microservice ratio 21.5%→14.3% as M grows; optimal hits 80% interception at δ=0.5 vs. sidecar needing ~160% of resources). 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.03151
192. **Park & Dagher, "Adaptive Honeypot Allocation in Multi-Attacker Networks via Bayesian Stackelberg Games"** — full text read via arXiv HTML. **CORRECTION to iteration-1 note:** this paper has NO reference to the Purdue Model, IEC 62443, or any OT/ICS concept anywhere in intro/related-work/system-model. The "layered structure" is generic graph-distance layering (nodes grouped by shortest-path distance to target, value discounted by α^distance) used purely for MIP tractability via backward induction — not an industrial reference architecture. Scales to 500 nodes / 1,500+ edges. Venue not confirmed as Computers & Security in the text read — treat as arXiv:2505.16043 until verified. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16043

    **Update, 8 November 2026 — 🟢 published version read; the correction above holds for the preprint only.** arXiv holds only the version of 21 May 2025, and the correction above was made from it. The version of record differs: *Computers & Security* 168 (2026) 104949, the September 2026 issue (received 24 January 2026, accepted 27 April 2026, online 12 May 2026; open access under CC BY 4.0; DOI 10.1016/j.cose.2026.104949; #247). It was read in full on 8 November 2026 from the ScienceDirect page and the PDF the user supplied.
    - **Purdue graphs:** its scalability runs (§7.4) use "hierarchical industrial-style networks generated by the Purdue model": a synthetic generator of six strictly layered levels from entry to target, with edges only from one level to the next, widened up to about 5,000 nodes.
    - **Level importance:** its candidate pruning scales the number of candidates kept per level by "normalized level importance and level width". The levels do not enter the MILP's objective.
    - **Other experiments:** the main ones use a generic 100-node directed graph. Nodes are generic components and exploits are ten abstract types; node values are distance-discounted target values (α = 0.6).
    - **Absent:** IEC 62443, zones, conduits, security levels and ATT&CK appear nowhere in the text.
    - **Baselines:** greedy on node value, random, a one-step lookahead (BMCL) and tabular Q-learning; no centrality baseline.
    - **The paper's own comparison tables** (Tables 1 and 4) have no OT, ICS or Purdue column, and its Algorithm 1 takes no level input.
    - **Code:** the data-availability statement puts it in a private GitHub repository for peer review (github.com/Youngboise/adaptive-honeypot-allocation). Whether it is public now is unchecked.
    - **Tier:** proposed T2 under A17's protocol, pending the user's confirmation.
    - **Stale until the verdict and its decisions-log entry:** three statements, for the version of record. They are the headline correction in `iteration-2-findings.md`, entry 8 of `01-thesis-citation-shortlist.md`, and the "Enterprise IT / generic networks" row of `research-synthesis-implementation.md`.

    **Update, 11 November 2026 — 🟢 T2 confirmed (D35).** The user confirmed the proposed T2 on 11 November 2026. The clause-by-clause mapping is in `01-search-screening-record.md` §3. The three statements named above as stale are corrected in D35's batch: `iteration-2-findings.md`, entry 8 of `01-thesis-citation-shortlist.md`, and `research-synthesis-implementation.md`.

## Iteration 3 Sources

### H. Probabilistic attack graphs / bi-level optimization (Kamhoua/Fu research cluster — deepened)
233. **Ma, Han, Leslie, Kamhoua, Fu** (2023) — "Optimal Decoy Resource Allocation for Proactive Defense in Probabilistic Attack Graphs," arXiv:2301.01336 (self-cites as AAMAS 2023, IFAAMAS, 8pp). Bi-level optimization (Stackelberg-style), proven NP-hard in general; reduces to a constrained optimization problem under assumptions, solved via a novel incentive-design projected-gradient-ascent method. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.01336
234. **⚠️ Bibliographic discrepancy to resolve:** a near-identically-scoped paper — "Optimal Resource Allocation for Proactive Defense with Deception in Probabilistic Attack Graphs" — is cited by a JHU faculty-publications page as **GameSec 2023 (Springer)**, and an NSF-hosted preprint (PAR) lists only **4 authors (Ma, Han, Kamhoua, Fu — no Nandi Leslie)**, vs. #233's 5-author arXiv version. Possibly a workshop/full-paper pair, possibly a citation error upstream — verify against both PDFs before citing either. 🟡 https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10496990
235. Li, Ma, Han, Fu (2022) — "Synthesis of Proactive Sensor Placement In Probabilistic Attack Graphs," arXiv:2210.07385. Same cluster; sensor placement (not decoys) combined with MTD — allocates intrusion detectors + stealthy sensors in two steps. 🟡 https://par.nsf.gov//servlets/purl/10411376
236. **Kulkarni, Cohen, Kamhoua, Fu** — "Integrated Resource Allocation and Strategy Synthesis in Safety Games on Graphs with Deception," arXiv:2407.14436. Places "traps" and "fake targets" via a hypergame model; proves the decoy-placement objective is monotone and, in certain cases, sub-/super-modular → **greedy algorithm with a (1−1/e)-approximation guarantee**. ⭐ Best theoretical anchor found so far for justifying a *greedy* method as more than "greedy because it's simple." 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2407.14436

Argues traditional centrality (degree/betweenness/closeness/eigenvector) **fails** to capture source/intermediate/target node roles in lateral-movement scenarios and proposes a role-aware replacement. ⭐ Directly complicates "centrality-based placement" as a simple baseline. 🟡 https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/phsmap/v658y2025ics0378437124007647.html

### L. AI-explanation-layer precedent — directly relevant to the secondary research question
244. **Podder, Ganim, Sreedharan, Ray, Ray** (Colorado State University) — "i-EXAM: Instructable and Explainable Attack Connectivity Graph Modeler," arXiv:2607.05888, built on the SPEAR framework (Podder et al. 2025). Compiles a network's Attack Connectivity Graph into a PDDL planning problem to identify attack paths and generate diverse *hardening* strategies with soundness/completeness guarantees, then uses an LLM **purely to explain** the strategies in natural language. ⭐ The closest architectural match found anywhere for "deterministic optimizer decides, LLM only explains" — just for general hardening, not deception placement. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.05888
245. "Auto-Prov" — attack-graph assistant (LLaMA-3-based), arXiv:2603.17100. Explicitly restricted to a "post-detection layer": converts detected attack graphs into human-readable narratives; does not perform detection or decide anything itself. Reinforces that the decide/explain split is a recognized, deliberate design pattern in current security research. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.17100
246. "Game-Theoretic and Machine Learning-based Approaches for Defensive Deception: A Survey," arXiv:2101.10121. Broader map of the game-theoretic deception-on-attack-graphs space (Durkota, Kulkarni, Milani, Tsemogne, Wagener, Aggarwal et al.). 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2101.10121

## Iteration 4 Sources

### O. Operational risk / false-positive cost — the previously-unresolved thread, now resolved
250. **D3O-IIoT** — "Deep reinforcement learning-driven dynamic deception orchestration for industrial IoT security." Dueling DQN agent with a multi-objective reward explicitly balancing attack mitigation, deception engagement, **false positive control**, and resource cost; ablation shows false-positive control is the single most important reward term (**51.4% performance degradation when removed**); achieves 13.7% attack mitigation at 0.3% false alarm across three IIoT datasets. ⭐ Best quantitative precedent found anywhere for formalizing the "operational risk" term in your composite objective — this is a real, ablation-tested number, not a guess. 🟢 https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12816736/

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢 full text read** (journal PDF and PMC printout supplied by you; full record at #324, which is *Scientific Reports* 16:2389 (2026), published online 21 December 2025). The reward is r = 0.70·A + 0.20·D − 0.06·F − 0.04·C, where F = 1 when a deception action other than no-op is applied to benign traffic. Table 6, as AMR / DSR / FAR / composite with composite = AMR − 0.5·FAR + 0.1·DSR: full model 12.4 / 3.4 / 2.0 / 11.76; no false-positive penalty 6.0 / 0.3 / 0.9 / 5.59; no cost penalty 14.7 / 4.7 / 3.8 / 13.28; equal weights 11.0 / 5.2 / 3.1 / 9.97. **The ⭐ judgement above is superseded.** The paper gives the false-positive penalty a low weight (0.06) and credits its low penalty weights with allowing "aggressive defense". Removing the term cut mitigation, not precision: the false-alarm rate fell from 2.0% to 0.9%. So it shows that such a term matters when present, not that it should be weighted heavily, and the term concerns decoy actions on benign traffic, which maps to Filter 1's malice-fidelity criterion rather than to δ·Risk. D5's evidence sentence needs a correction entry in the decisions log; δ's default and sweep are unchanged and rest on #272's sweep practice alone.
251. "Strategic Deployment of Honeypots in Blockchain-based IoT Systems," arXiv:2405.12951. Directly compares deployment-strategy utility **with and without false-positive penalties**, and studies how varying deployment cost and penalty magnitude affects strategy utility. A second, independent formal precedent for putting a number on false-positive cost inside a placement/deployment utility function. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2405.12951

### P. The Ma/Han/Kamhoua/Fu discrepancy — fully resolved
252. **Ma, Han, Kamhoua, Fu (2023)** — "Optimal Resource Allocation for Proactive Defense with Deception in Probabilistic Attack Graphs," **GameSec 2023, Springer LNCS vol. 14167, pp. 215–233**, DOI 10.1007/978-3-031-50670-3_11. **This is the confirmed, published, peer-reviewed version — 4 authors, no Nandi Leslie.** Cite this one, not the arXiv preprint. 🟢 https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-031-50670-3_11 (also indexed at https://researchr.org/conferenceseries/gamesec/publications)
253. The GameSec 2023 proceedings TOC surfaced two more cluster papers not previously catalogued: "Asymmetric Centrality Game Against Network Epidemic Propagation" (Kouam, Hayel, Deugoue, Tsemogne, Kamhoua, pp. 86–109) and "FlipPath Game to Counter Stealthy Attacks in SDN-Based Tactical Networks" (Mvah, Tchendji, Djamégni, Anwar, Tosh, Kamhoua, pp. 294–308). 🟡 https://researchr.org/conferenceseries/gamesec/publications
254. Wan, Cho, Zhu, Anwar, Kamhoua, Singh — "Optimizing Effectiveness and Defense of Drone Surveillance Missions via Honey Drones," *ACM Trans. Internet Technol.* 24 (2024); companion "Deception in Drone Surveillance Missions: Strategic vs. Learning Approaches," MobiHoc 2023. Same cluster's domain-specific deception-*placement* work — this time for UAV swarms. Reinforces the pattern: this exact group has now done placement optimization for AD networks, microservices, epidemic-control networks, and drones — never OT. 🟡 https://ci.lib.ncsu.edu/profiles/mzhu5

### Q. AI-explanation-layer precedent, deepened — now with a direct OT link
255. **Podder, Sreedharan, Caglar, Ray, Bashir, Ray** — "SPEAR: Security Posture Evaluation using AI Planner-Reasoning on Attack-Connectivity Hypergraphs," SACMAT 2025 (ACM), arXiv:2506.01227. The full formal framework underlying i-EXAM (iteration 3, #244): converts network configs + vulnerability descriptions into PDDL planning models automatically, generates diverse hardening strategies with soundness/completeness guarantees. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.01227

(2021)** — "An Automatic Planning-Based Attack Path Discovery Approach from IT to OT Networks," *Security and Communication Networks* (Wiley), DOI 10.1155/2021/1444182. ⭐⭐ The missing direct link: uses the *same* PDDL-based automated-planning approach as SPEAR/i-EXAM, applied specifically to **IT-to-OT attack path discovery**, with a device-reachability graph-partitioning algorithm for scalability. Not about deception — but confirms the exact planning formalism your AI-explanation layer would sit on top of has already been shown to work on OT-adjacent topologies. Worth citing to bridge the "AI explains" architecture directly into your OT context. 🟢 https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1155/2021/1444182

### R. Dynamic/temporal placement — a different framing worth knowing about
259. Huang & Zhu (NYU) — "Farsighted Risk Mitigation of Lateral Movement Using Dynamic Cognitive Honeypots," GameSec 2020. Honeypots relocate randomly over time (not a one-time static placement); formalizes a Long-Term Vulnerability metric and three tradeoffs (interference probability, stealthiness, roaming cost). Your project is scoped as static placement, but worth one sentence in related-work acknowledging the dynamic alternative exists and why you're not doing it (weekend-hours scope, testbed complexity). 🟢 https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2007.13981
260. Al Amin, Shetty, Njilla, Tosh, Kamhoua (2021) — "Hidden Markov Model and Cyber Deception for the Prevention of Adversarial Lateral Movement," *IEEE Access* 9:49662–49682. Confirmed: two-phase approach — HMM predicts the most likely attack path, then a Partially Observable Monte-Carlo Planning (POMCP) framework deploys decoys along it and adapts if the attacker deviates. 🟢 https://doaj.org/article/0b8e5fc46fba4948a5a5248feae8d1b7
261. "Compact Representation of Value Function in Partially Observable Stochastic Games," arXiv:1903.05511. Formalizes a "Lateral Movement POSG" on a DAG — likely foundational context for source #238 (Decoy Allocation Against Lateral Movement), whose own primary source still couldn't be isolated this iteration; probably needs IEEE Xplore/database access rather than open web search. 🟡 https://arxiv.org/pdf/1903.05511

### S. Honeypot detectability — a new, unprompted but relevant thread
262. "Time-to-Lie: Identifying Industrial Control System Honeypots Using the Internet Control Message Protocol," arXiv:2410.17731. Real-world attackers can fingerprint ICS honeypots via IP TTL values alone. Directly relevant to "candidate deception locations": a decoy that's trivially detectable at a heavily-scanned position may be worse than no decoy there. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2410.17731

EuroS&P 2025 paper on exposed OT/ICS measurement — notes ~90% of exposed ICS devices show fewer than 10 open ports, a heuristic researchers use to separate honeypots from real devices at internet scale. Same detectability theme as #262. 🟢 https://gsmaragd.github.io/publications/EuroSP2025-ICS/EuroSP2025-ICS.pdf

## Iteration 5 Sources

### V. IEC 62443 Security Level vectors — resolved, with a genuine usable structure
267. **IEC 62443-3-3, Annex A ("Discussion of the SL vector")** — a zone or conduit's security level is formally a **7-element vector**, one score (0–4) per Foundational Requirement (FR1 Identification & Authentication, FR2 Use Control, FR3 System Integrity, FR4 Data Confidentiality, FR5 Restricted Data Flow, FR6 Timely Response to Events, FR7 Resource Availability) — e.g. SL=(3,3,3,1,2,1,3). ⭐ A genuinely usable, standardized numeric structure you could adapt as a criticality-weighting input, rather than inventing your own criticality score from scratch. 🟢 https://syc-se.iec.ch/deliveries/cybersecurity-guidelines/security-standards-and-best-practices/iec-62443/
268. **exida (functional-safety/OT-security consultancy)** — explicit industry admission: **"a pragmatic means of SL quantification has not yet been developed and vetted by the community. One can apply a semi-quantitative approach, however."** ⭐ Important for scoping and novelty framing: even basic SL quantification is an acknowledged open problem, which (a) means you should present any SL-based weighting you build as a reasonable semi-quantitative approximation, not a solved measurement, and (b) makes it even less likely anyone has built a formal placement optimizer on top of it — reinforcing the novelty gap one level deeper than iteration 1/3 established. 🟢 https://www.exida.com/Blog/iec-62443-levels-levels-and-more-levels
269. "A cybersecurity risk assessment methodology for industrial automation control systems," *Int'l J. Information Security* (Springer, 2025). Performs formal **attack-path analysis within IEC 62443-3-2's zone/conduit requirement 5 (ZCR5)** to select countermeasures — not deception placement, but the closest thing found anywhere to "someone formally reasoning over attack paths using 62443 zone/conduit structure." Needs direct engagement in your differentiation section, same treatment as Zambianco et al. 🟡 https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10207-025-00990-9
270. Hollerer, Sauter, Kastner (2022) — "Risk Assessments Considering Safety, Security, and Their Interdependencies in OT Environments," ARES 2022 (ACM). Combines CVSS + IEC 62443 SL + IEC 61508 SIL into one composite risk methodology over zones/conduits. Good precedent for *how* to justify combining heterogeneous metrics into one score — directly useful methodological model for your own composite-objective writeup. 🟢 https://dl.acm.org/doi/fullHtml/10.1145/3538969.3543814

### W. Qin et al. — fully resolved
271. **Qin, Jiang, Dong, Doss (2024) — method confirmed.** It is a bio-inspired adaptive defense framework (dissimilar redundancy + diversity) that mixes **optimal network shuffling (MTD)** with cyber deception to maximize attacker dwell time on decoys, plus **dual heterogeneous regenerable subnets** for availability protection; evaluated on an SDN platform in a simulated industrial manufacturing network. **This is not a placement-optimization paper** — it's an MTD+deception hybrid mechanism paper, methodologically distinct from what your project does. Cite it for "ICS-specific hybrid defense exists" context, not as competing placement-optimization work. 🟢 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1016/j.cose.2023.103506

### X. False-positive/cost coefficient derivation — resolved (practice, not a formula)
272. Park & Dagher's own paper runs an explicit **sensitivity sweep on honeypot cost** (their Fig. 4, cost varied 0→10), reporting how defender utility and optimal strategy shift as the coefficient changes. This is representative of the field's standard practice: **parametric sensitivity sweeps, not formal expert elicitation.** A broader search for expert-elicitation methodology applied to honeypot/deception cost coefficients turned up nothing in this literature — the technique is common in health economics and risk management generally, but not used here. Practical implication: your brief's own plan ("weighting justified through literature, experiments, sensitivity analysis") already matches actual community practice — lean into sensitivity sweeps as your primary justification method rather than trying to construct a formal elicitation process. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.16043

### Y. Cluster bibliography, further deepened
273. Additional ICS honeypot tools newly catalogued (not in iterations 1–4): **HoneyVP** (You, Lv, Sun, Wen, Sun, ICC 2021 — cost-effective hybrid ICS honeypot architecture), **ICSpot** (Conti, Trolese, Turrin, 2022 — high-interaction ICS honeypot), Trend Micro's "Caught in the Act" realistic factory honeypot report, Abe, Tanaka, Uchida, Horata (2021) — traceback honeypot for ICS networks, SICE J. Control Meas. Syst. Integr. 11(4):372–379. 🟡

278. **"Optimally Blending Honeypots into Production Networks: Hardness and Algorithms,"** Springer (NSF-funded, Colorado State Bill 18-086). Frames placement as an indistinguishability/**blending** problem rather than attack-path coverage — honeypots are assigned IP addresses among real production hosts at a cost, and the paper proves hardness via a reduction from the Subset Product problem. ⭐ A genuinely distinct methodological angle from everything catalogued so far, and directly relevant to the honeypot-detectability thread from iteration 4 (#262/263) — coverage-optimal placement and blending-optimal placement are different objectives that could conflict, worth a sentence acknowledging the distinction in your problem formulation. 🟢 https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-031-45933-7_17

### BB. Cluster bibliography — further confirmed, no new OT angle

A third treatment of the same probabilistic-attack-graph decoy-allocation line of work first seen in iteration 3 (arXiv 2023) and iteration 4 (GameSec 2023 Springer) — confirms this is an actively maintained, still-non-OT research thread rather than a one-off. 🟡

Explicitly designed to be adaptive, scalable, and stage-aware (aware of which phase of an intrusion is underway) — deepens iteration 1's original citation without changing its conclusion (still enterprise/generic, not OT). 🟢 https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1389128625009478

### CC. OT asset criticality — a genuine numeric precedent, independent of the SL-vector route
282. **"Criticality analysis of attack graphs"** (US patent). ⭐⭐ Gives an actual worked formula for OT/SCADA asset criticality: **Criticality = f(locality, centrality, damage)**, where locality is the asset's Purdue level (e.g., Level 2/3), centrality is a network-connectivity measure (e.g., bytes exchanged with other assets), and damage is the fraction of operational load the asset controls (e.g., 40%) — combined via a stated equation into a single criticality number (worked example: SCADA-2 → criticality 1.239). This is independent of, and complementary to, the IEC 62443 SL-vector route from iteration 5 — you now have two different legitimate ways to justify a criticality term, one standards-based (SL vectors) and one graph/operational-based (this formula), and could even cite both and note they capture different things. 🟢 https://image-ppubs.uspto.gov/dirsearch-public/print/downloadPdf/11252175

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢 full text read** (granted patent supplied by you; record at #325). Equation (1) is additive, C(i) = α·l(i) + β·c(i) + γ·d(i): locality from Purdue position, centrality a degree-and-strength measure over packet-share weights (the patent mentions betweenness but does not use it), damage from share of operational load; the SCADA-2 example gives 1.239. A separate risk equation multiplies probability by criticality, R(i) = p(i) × C(i). This project's Crit(v) multiplies centrality by damage, and three repository statements misdescribe this source: D2's "multiplied by", the research synthesis's "locality × centrality × damage", and `formal-problem-definition.md` §2's claim that betweenness follows "the pattern in source 282". Opens A40. Because its centrality reads edge weights as traffic, it also bears on A35.

**Iteration 6 headline: the centrality/lateral-movement thread is now closed (conclusion: needs institutional access, not more searching) rather than left dangling, and this round's best find — a worked OT criticality formula combining Purdue-level locality, network centrality, and operational damage — gives you a second, independent way to justify the criticality term in your composite objective. No new OT-structured placement-optimization work surfaced; the novelty claim is unchanged after six iterations.**

He answers "which layer of which device, and which tactic, given attacker psychology"; you answer "which assets across a network graph, using formal multi-metric optimization." Same phrase, different question — cite this explicitly and distinguish it, or a reviewer who knows this chapter may think you've either missed it or conflated the two. 🟢 https://faculty.nps.edu/ncrowe/Designing_Deceptions_for_Protecting_Industrial_Control_Systems.pdf

## Iteration 9 Sources — continuing deliberate diversification (DTIC thesis corpus, a federal analysis tool, and a first European entry)

### HH. A federal attack-path/criticality analysis tool — methodological template, not a deception tool
299. **MITRE — CICAT (Critical Infrastructure Cyberspace Analysis Tool)**, developed 2019 for an IAEA Coordinated Research Project on nuclear-facility cybersecurity, v2.0 licensable. ⭐⭐ Uses MITRE ATT&CK-based attack-path analysis between entry points and targets, computes impact/risk scores per attack path from asset criticality scores, and identifies **"hot spots" and "hot links"** — infrastructure locations an adversary is likely to transit or dwell. **Important distinction:** CICAT does *not* place deception assets — it's a risk-assessment and incident-response-planning tool, not a decoy-placement optimizer. But its underlying engine (attack-path generation + criticality-weighted impact scoring + hot-spot identification, all on MITRE ATT&CK) is structurally very close to what your project's *analysis layer* needs before the optimizer runs, and it's already been validated on nuclear-facility-class critical infrastructure. Worth citing as evidence the underlying analysis techniques are mature and federally validated, while being explicit that CICAT stops short of deception placement — that's your contribution. 🟢 https://www.mitre.org/our-impact/intellectual-property/critical-infrastructure-cyberspace-analysis-tool-cicattm
300. Cyber-Physical System Security With Deceptive Virtual Hosts for Industrial Control Networks (OSTI.gov journal article). Self-configuring honeypots using Ettercap for passive network-entity discovery, feeding a novel four-step algorithm for autonomous Honeyd configuration. An automated *deployment* algorithm — not attack-path-coverage optimization, but a genuine precedent for automating the mechanics of decoy setup once locations are chosen. 🟢 https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/1136315
301. Sandia — HADES deployed for **NJ TRANSIT GRID** (a named, real U.S. transit agency), using SDN, cloud computing, and agentless VM introspection to migrate attackers into a canary environment mid-attack. A concrete, named real-world OT deception deployment case study. 🟢 https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/1780582

### II. A first European entry
302. **ENISA — "Proactive Detection of Security Incidents: Honeypots"** (2012; the agency has run this program since). Tested 30 different standalone honeypots (SCADA included) across a formal taxonomy (server-side, client-side, sandboxes); written for European CERTs. General-purpose, not OT-specific, and doesn't address placement — but a legitimate EU-regulatory-body citation alongside CISA/FBI/NCSC (iteration 7) and Sandia (iteration 8), useful for showing the thesis draws on multiple national/regional authorities, not just US sources. 🟢 https://www.enisa.europa.eu/sites/default/files/publications/ENISA_Honeypots_study.pdf
303. **securiCAD® / EU "CyberWiz" project** (Horizon 2020-funded; developed by foreseeti AB, a KTH Royal Institute of Technology spin-off, Sweden). Models complex networks, proactively simulates attacks, discovers vulnerabilities and their dependencies, and visualizes **critical attack paths to designated targets** with time-to-compromise estimates. Another close-but-distinct precedent — attack-path and criticality simulation for critical infrastructure, European-funded, commercial — not deception-focused, but same analytical family as CICAT [299]. 🟡 https://www.cordis.europa.eu/article/id/132895

### JJ. The DTIC/NPS thesis corpus — confirmed as a large, comparable-scope body of prior graduate work
304. A targeted DTIC search surfaced a **substantial corpus of NPS Master's theses** on ICS/OT honeypots beyond Rowe's own papers (iteration 8), spanning roughly a decade: cost-effective/targetable ICS honeypot construction, building-automation-system honeypots, SIEM-integrated honeypots, a honeypot intrusion-detection framework ("HoneyHive"), and **Dougherty (2020), "Evasion of Honeypot Detection Mechanisms through Improved Interactivity of ICS-Based Systems"** (advised by Nguyen and Rowe) — directly relevant to the detectability thread from iterations 4 and 8. This is a large, directly-comparable body of prior M.S.-level thesis work at a peer institution — useful less for individual citations than as a scope/rigor benchmark: none of these theses attempt formal placement optimization either; they're all deployment/hardening/evasion-focused, reinforcing the same gap from yet another angle. 🟡 (index via https://apps.dtic.mil, search terms: "industrial control system honeypot")

**Iteration 9 headline: CICAT [299] and securiCAD [303] are this round's best finds — both are federally/EU-validated attack-path-and-criticality analysis engines that stop just short of deception placement, making them the clearest "here's the mature technique, here's the gap I'm filling" pairing found in nine iterations. ENISA [302] gives you a genuine second regulatory-body citation. The DTIC sweep also confirms, from yet another independent body of work (a decade of NPS theses), that nobody doing empirical ICS honeypot research has attempted formal placement optimization either — reinforcing the novelty claim from a fourth independent direction (after the academic optimization literature, the commercial vendors, and Sandia).**

## Iteration 10 Sources — sector-specific pass (electric utility / smart grid)

### KK. The closest OT-specific placement-optimization paper found in ten iterations
305. **Devika Jay (2023) — "Deception Technology Based Intrusion Protection and Detection Mechanism for Digital Substations: A Game Theoretical Approach,"** *IEEE Access* 11:53301–53314, DOI 10.1109/ACCESS.2023.3279504. ⭐⭐⭐ **Read this one closely before writing section 14.** Formulates decoy *allocation* as a bi-level optimization inside a single-leader single-follower (Stackelberg) defender-attacker game over a GOOSE-message VLAN in a digital substation (IEC 61850). Considers both simultaneous and sequential allocation of protection-decoys and detection-decoys, proves equilibrium existence, and validates on 3-IED and 12-IED test systems against real PSRC-I5 protection-relay data, benchmarked against a zero-sum game baseline. This is a formally optimized, published, peer-reviewed, OT-specific decoy-placement paper — the closest thing to direct prior art found across all ten iterations, closer than Zambianco et al. (enterprise IT) or DecIED/Decepti-SCADA (not formally optimized). **Real, defensible distinctions from your project, but they need to be argued explicitly, not assumed:** (a) scoped to *one substation's internal VLAN*, not a multi-zone architecture spanning IT/OT boundary, DMZ, engineering workstation, and control LAN; (b) tied to *IEC 61850/GOOSE protocol semantics* specifically, not *IEC 62443 zone/conduit* structure generically; (c) the objective is *game-theoretic equilibrium over decoy type* (protection vs. detection), not a *composite of coverage + early detection + criticality − operational risk − cost* with criticality and risk as explicit, independently weighted terms. This paper earns a dedicated paragraph in your differentiation section, not a passing citation. 🟢 https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10132467/

### LL. Sector-specific (electric utility) gap confirmations — a fourth type of independent source
306. **EPRI — "Deception Technology: Emerging Cyber Security Technology for Utilities"** (2019), an Electric Power Research Institute industry report. ⭐⭐ States plainly: *"There are many practical unknowns about the technology in terms of the appropriate design and placement of lures and decoys to deliver high-fidelity alerts."* A sector-authoritative body — the electric utility industry's own collaborative R&D organization — naming the placement gap explicitly, independent of academic surveys, national labs, or EU agencies. This is now the fourth distinct *type* of source (after academic literature, Sandia, and NPS theses) converging on the same conclusion. 🟢 https://restservice.epri.com/publicdownload/000000003002017417/0/Product
307. "Security Aspects of Internet of Things aided Smart Grids: a Bibliometric Survey" — states directly that deception technology is "seldom used" in smart-grid security literature, and that existing smart-grid honeypot systems have only "preliminary" realism/scalability evaluation, explicitly naming this a research gap. A sector-specific (smart grid) echo of the general-OT gap statements already on file, from an independent survey. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2005.00915

### MM. Additional sector-specific tools and projects
308. **SDN-microSENSE** (EU Horizon 2020 project; consortium including Schneider Electric, Atos, Norwegian University of Science and Technology, Leibniz Universität Hannover) — deploys "Electrical Power and Energy Systems (EPES) Honeypots" plus a Honeypot Manager for smart-grid risk assessment. ## Targeted Check — LLM-assisted plausibility-scoring grounding (D12 follow-up)

Run specifically to ground the plausibility-scoring-assist design (D12) in real methodology precedent, not just internal reasoning — bounded, not a broad pass.

318. **"Uncovering Vulnerabilities of LLM-Assisted Cyber Threat Intelligence," arXiv:2509.23573 (Feb 2026).** ⭐⭐ States directly that standard LLM-as-a-judge approaches are unreliable for automatic classification, since models tend to rationalize their own outputs and fail to critically evaluate contradictions — proposes an iterative human-in-the-loop framework instead (LLM classifies, human review targets uncertain/novel cases). Direct, citable justification for the human-confirmation gate already built into `candidate_locations` — this isn't a conservative design choice, it's addressing a documented, named failure mode. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/html/2509.23573v3
319. "Expert-in-the-Loop Systems with Cross-Domain and In-Domain Few-Shot Learning for Software Vulnerability Detection." Confidence-based routing directs human review specifically to high-uncertainty cases rather than reviewing everything uniformly. Optional refinement for the review workflow in `02-ai-role.md` §10 — prioritize assets where the AI hedges ("mostly") over clean yes/no cases — not adopted yet, worth a sentence either way. 🟢 *Authors and URL added 1 October 2026:* Farr, Talty, Farr, Stockdale, Cruickshank, West, arXiv:2506.10104 (June 2025). https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10104 *(D26: under the four-point scale there is no "mostly" hedge to route on. The plant-scale design in `02-ai-role.md` §10 routes instead on boundary answers, on answers the AI flags as unsure, and on a random audit sample.)*
320. **"ChatGPT and Other Large Language Models for Cybersecurity of Smart Grid Applications," arXiv:2311.05462.** LLM-plus-human-in-the-loop applied specifically to GOOSE/SV digital-substation communications — the same IEC 61850 domain as the Devika Jay paper (#305/312). Different task (anomaly detection, not plausibility scoring), but confirms LLM-assisted analysis has real precedent in exactly this OT-substation communication context, not just generic cybersecurity. 🟢
321. "Human-in-the-Loop for Machine Learning in Offensive Cybersecurity" (ORCHID). Precedent for keeping an audit trail of AI reasoning alongside its suggestions ("run-cards, prompts, evidence") — matches the `ai_reasoning` column already in the schema, not a new design need. 🟡
322. "CyBOKClaw: Human-in-the-Loop CyBOK Mapping." Frames LLM output explicitly as a "candidate generator for expert review," not a final classification — same framing already used for plausibility-scoring-assist, found independently rather than copied from this paper. 🟡

**Verdict: the D12 design holds up under scrutiny — strengthens the methodology section, doesn't require rework. The one real, citable finding worth having in the thesis is #318's specific failure-mode evidence for why LLM classification needs human confirmation, not just general caution.**

310.
