Full mathematical text remains inaccessible via open web (IEEE Xplore blocked the direct fetch); still needs NFSU library proxy access for the actual formulation. 🟡

    **Update, 12 October 2026 (D28).** Superseded by #312: the full text was later read. The NFSU library route named here is not available (A2).

311. **US Patent 12,425,450 B2 — "Method for securing digital substations and system thereof"** — Devika Jay, Himanshu Goyel, Amulya Sreejith, Rajesh Rathi; assigned to **GridSentry Private Limited (Karnataka, India)**; filed Feb 22, 2024, claiming priority from Indian application 202341012168, filed Feb 22, 2023 — the same year as the IEEE Access paper. ⭐⭐⭐ This is not just an academic paper — this exact research line has been **commercialized by an Indian company**. The patent's mechanism is methodologically distinct from the academic paper's: it's a *reactive switching* system — a decoy network mirrors the real substation network, and on detecting malicious activity, a switching node redirects the attacker to it using periodically-changing bogus MAC IDs — versus the paper's *proactive bi-level optimization* over which individual IEDs get decoys ahead of time. Same inventor, same domain, two different technical approaches, both now needing acknowledgment: cite the patent as evidence this research line has reached commercial deployment and IP protection in India specifically; cite the paper for the formal optimization method. 🟢 https://patentsgazette.uspto.gov/week38/OG/html/1538-4/US12425450-20250923.html

**This targeted check (one paper, not a broad pass) surfaced something a full iteration might not have: a live Indian commercial entity building on adjacent technology. That's now part of the answer to "who else is working on this," not just "what papers exist."**

---

## Full-Text Verification — Devika Jay, confirmed from the primary source

312. **Devika Jay (2023) — full text now read.** ⭐⭐⭐ Confirms and sharpens the distinction drawn from the abstract alone. The paper solves a **graph-observability problem, not a site-selection problem**: the GOOSE VLAN is a strongly-connected bidirectional graph, provably unobservable by a single decoy (any digraph needs out-degree ≤1 at every vertex to be observable, and this one doesn't). The core contribution is decomposing that graph into P(n) = 2 + n(n−3)/2 minimal **"observable subgraphs"** — two cyclic rings (clockwise/counter-clockwise GOOSE flow) plus per-node inward/outward star subgraphs — that together guarantee **full** observability with far fewer decoys than covering every individual path would need (2 vs. 6 decoys for a 3-IED VLAN; 56 vs. 132 for the 12-IED PSRC-I5 test case). The bi-level Stackelberg game then allocates *protection-vs-detection decoy type* across those already-determined subgraph slots — it does not choose *which* assets receive a decoy from a larger candidate pool the way this project's problem does. The reward functions are built entirely from GOOSE/substation-specific cyber-physical dynamics — Cruz's network-calculus delay bounds (arrival/service curves) and a voltage-state residual for a false-trip scenario — protocol-specific machinery with no IEC 62443, no MITRE ATT&CK, no asset criticality, and no multi-asset attack-path modeling anywhere in it. **Directly citable:** the paper's own conclusion states extending from the GOOSE VLAN to the SV VLAN — a second VLAN *within the same substation* — is still future work, and real-testbed deployment is still future work too. That is the authors' own words confirming the scope boundary, not an inference. 🟢 (full text on file)

**Net effect: this closes the Jay thread. The full text makes the project's differentiation from it stronger and easier to state than the abstract-only version did — this is a resource-allocation-under-guaranteed-full-observability problem on one VLAN's message graph, not a budget-constrained, multi-metric, attack-path-driven site-selection problem across a multi-zone OT architecture. No further chasing of this paper is needed.**

---

## Targeted Sector Check — Water & Manufacturing (closing the last flagged risk)

Run specifically to close the item named in `research-synthesis-implementation.md`'s "What Could Still Change This Picture": whether a water- or manufacturing-sector equivalent of the Jay paper exists, the way the electric-utility pivot in iteration 10 found one.

313. **Water sector — no placement-optimization paper found, and the surrounding ecosystem is less mature than electric utility's.** EPA's own water-sector cybersecurity guidance (2023, revised Aug 2024) covers asset inventory, MFA, patching, and vendor management — no mention of deception technology as a category at all. A GAO report (Aug 2024) states EPA "has not conducted a comprehensive sector-wide risk assessment" for water. Where electric utility had EPRI publish a dedicated *deception technology* report in 2019 naming placement as an open question, water doesn't yet have an equivalent report to even raise the question. This is a stronger negative result than "not found" — the sector isn't at the maturity level where this gap would be expected to be filled yet. 🟢
314. A water-treatment-plant "cyber-twin" honeypot (arXiv, Sept 2025) — a 6-stage WTP digital twin used for honeypot fidelity, not placement optimization. Adds to the tools inventory, doesn't change the gap. 🟢
315. **Manufacturing — the closest sector-specific find, but needs the same full-text scrutiny Jay's paper got before being treated as resolved.** Shahin, Maghanaki, Chen (2026), "Evaluating Synthetic Cyber Deception Strategies Under Uncertainty via Game Theory Approach," *Sensors* (MDPI) 26(6):1748 — published in a Smart Manufacturing and Industrial Cybersecurity special issue, March 2026. Formal Bayesian Stackelberg game framework introducing two new metrics ("value of deception," "price of transparency") for decoy allocation optimization, with derived bounds and break-even conditions. Based on abstract and keyword evidence only (no full text obtained this pass): appears to be a general analytical evaluation framework tested on synthetic/heterogeneous scenarios, not a concrete OT-network-topology or IEC-62443-structured placement system — but that distinction rests on secondary evidence, the same level Jay's paper had before its full read reshaped the picture. Likely open-access via MDPI, unlike the IEEE-gated papers — worth pulling the actual PDF before writing section 14's manufacturing-sector sentence. 🟡

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢 full text read** (PDF supplied by you). Authors Mohammad Shahin, Mazdak Maghanaki, Fengshan Frank Chen; the full title adds ": Linking Information Leakage and Game Outcomes in Cyber Deception"; doi:10.3390/s26061748. A Stackelberg game measured against a baseline through the value of deception and the price of transparency; it studies the trade-off between decoy quality and quantity and chooses decoy types greedily. There is no network topology, zone structure or set of positions. **Not a placement competitor**: closes A4 and D10's open sub-item, and retires the caution above.
316. Zhou, Almutairi, Alsenani, Ahmad (2023), "Honeypot Based Industrial Threat Detection Using Game Theory in Cyber-Physical System," *J. Grid Computing* 21(4):59 — industrial CPS honeypot game theory; available evidence suggests a focus on DDoS/MITM threat detection rather than placement optimization specifically. Method not independently confirmed. 🟡
317. Two more power-grid-specific game-theoretic honeypot papers surfaced while searching manufacturing, not water or manufacturing themselves: Diamantoulakis et al. (2020) "Game Theoretic Honeypot Deployment in Smart Grid," *Sensors* 20:4199, and Tian et al. (2020) "Prospect Theoretic Study of Honeypot Defense Against APT in Power Grid," *IEEE Access* 8:64075. Their appearance *while searching other sectors* is itself informative — electric/grid is disproportionately the best-studied OT sub-vertical for this exact method family, everywhere this check looked. 🟢

**Verdict: the novelty claim survives, with one item still open, not fully closed.** Water strengthens the picture — the sector isn't mature enough yet to have produced a competing paper. Manufacturing surfaces a real, very recent candidate [315] that looks — on abstract evidence — like it's solving a different, more abstract problem than this project's OT-topology-structured one, but "looks like" is exactly the phrase that turned out to be wrong about Jay's paper until the full text was read. Treat #315 the same way: get the PDF (likely free, MDPI is open-access) before section 14 states the manufacturing-sector comparison as settled.

---

    **Update, 11 November 2026 — A17 (D35).** Both papers named here were A17 records:
    - Tian et al. (2020), record 290, was read in full from the PDF the user supplied. Every bus node gets a honeypot, and the game picks only its mode (high or low interaction), using node values from electrical centrality. There is no choice of positions: **T1**.
    - Diamantoulakis et al. (2020), record 178, is **T1** from its abstract: the game chooses how many honeypots to run, not where.

## Post-Iteration-10 Additions (logged after TA-1 reference verification)

323. **Osman, Nadeem, Hemida, Kamhoua (2023)** — "Optimizing Honeypot Placement Strategies with Graph Neural Networks for Enhanced Resilience via Cyber Deception," *GNNet '23: Proc. 2nd Graph Neural Networking Workshop*, Paris, pp. 37–43, DOI 10.1145/3630049.3630169. Completes the bibliographic record for the previously-uncredited GNN placement entry. Note the author list: Kamhoua again — this is the same cluster that has now done placement optimization for AD networks, microservices, epidemic-control networks, drones, and GNN-based generic networks, and still never OT. That strengthens rather than weakens the gap argument. 🟢 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3630049.3630169

324. **Wushishi, Hussain, Khalid, Hussain, Jamjoom, Ullah (2025)** — "D3O-IIoT: deep reinforcement learning-driven dynamic deception orchestration for industrial IoT security," *Scientific Reports*, DOI 10.1038/s41598-025-33426-4. Completes the record for #250. Confirms the ablation figure: false-positive control is the dominant reward term. Article number still to be captured for the final reference list. 🟢 https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-025-33426-4

    **Update, 12 October 2026 (D28).** The article number is 2389 (*Scientific Reports* 16:2389, 2026; A8). "Confirms the ablation figure: false-positive control is the dominant reward term" is superseded by #250's 6 October update: the term's weight is 0.06, and its ablation shows only that the term matters when present.

325. **Hassanzadeh, Hasan, Nayak (2022)** — "Criticality analysis of attack graphs," US Patent 11,252,175 B2, assignee Accenture Global Solutions Ltd.; EP equivalent EP3644579B1, published 2022-03-30; continuation US11811816B2 (2023). Completes the record for #282. Exact US grant date still to be confirmed. 🟢 https://patents.google.com/patent/US11252175

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢** US grant date read from the patent itself: 15 February 2022. Inventors Amin Hassanzadeh, Kamrul Hasan, Anup Nayak. The formula is additive; see #282's update.

326. **Liu, Xu, Hu (2026)** — "Graph Neural Network-Guided TrapManager for Critical Path Identification and Decoy Deployment," *Mathematics* (MDPI), vol. 14, no. 4, art. 683, DOI 10.3390/math14040683. Guangzhou University Cyberspace Institute; Peng Cheng Laboratory; **Guangdong Key Laboratory of Industrial Control System Security**. ⭐⭐ **Full text read, not abstract.** Found by forward-citation chasing from #323, not by keyword search.

**What it does.** GAT learns risk-aware node embeddings on MulVAL vulnerability attack graphs, fusing CVSS-derived node scores with Node2Vec structural features; a path-MLP ranks source–target routes; a MIP then places "pointTraps" to maximise expected interception of high-weight paths minus deployment cost, under a budget, with mandatory coverage of the Top-K paths. Event-triggered re-optimisation on trap activation. Evaluated on 32-, 75- and 2030-node graphs plus Cora transfer.

**Its MIP, stated exactly:** max Σ p_k·q_k − λ·Σ c_j·x_j, s.t. Σ x_j ≤ B; q_k ≤ Σ_{j∈A_k} x_j; Σ_{j∈A_π(i)} x_j ≥ 1 for the Top-K.

**Assessment — does NOT break the novelty claim, and the reason is specific.** Its objective has two terms: weighted path coverage and deployment cost. This project's has five. Missing entirely: (a) no early-detection term — steps-to-intercept is *measured* in evaluation but never *optimised*; (b) no operational-risk / false-positive term — λ is deployment cost only, and per #250 that is the single most influential term in a comparable model; (c) path weights come from a learned GNN over CVSS severity, not from IEC 62443 SL vectors plus operational damage share; (d) no zones, conduits, Purdue levels, OT protocols, or ICS semantics anywhere — the graphs are CVE-driven IT attack graphs; (e) no candidate filtering at all: every node is placeable, with no plausibility rubric and no detectability check.

**But it does compress one claim.** The MIP *formulation shape* — binary placement variables, budget constraint, coverage linearisation q_k ≤ Σ x_j, cost penalty — is now clearly standard, and this paper uses very nearly the same one. Never claim the formulation as the contribution. The contribution is the inputs and the composition of terms, not the solver model. Zambianco already implied this; TrapManager makes it explicit.

**Four ways this paper is directly useful, not merely a competitor:**
1. Independent precedent for the coverage linearisation and budget constraint used in `02-optimization-formulation.md` — cite it as convergent practice rather than defending the formulation from scratch.
2. Its mandatory-Top-K-coverage constraint is structurally the same device as the optional per-zone minimum-coverage constraint in `formal-problem-definition.md` §6 — precedent for that constraint if it gets implemented.
3. It names concrete mitigations for MIP scalability at ~2000 nodes: restrict decision variables to a trigger-centred region, warm-start from the previous solution, impose solver time limits for near-optimal returns. That is a citable answer to the scalability open problem in `04-deployment-requirements.md` §7.
4. §5.2 states explicitly that trap-aware adversaries — decoy fingerprinting, policy adaptation across alerts — are **out of scope and a stated limitation**. That is precisely what this project's Filter 2 detectability check addresses. A 2026 paper naming the gap your candidate filter fills is a strong differentiation point for section 14.

**One more thing worth noting:** affiliation 3 is an Industrial Control System Security key laboratory, and the work still contains no OT structure. An ICS security lab doing decoy placement on generic CVE attack graphs is further evidence for the gap, not against it. 🟢 https://www.mdpi.com/2227-7390/14/4/683

    **Update, 12 October 2026 (D28).** Point (b)'s "per #250 that is the single most influential term" is superseded: #250's false-positive term weighs 0.06 and concerns deception actions on benign traffic (see its 6 October update). What still separates TrapManager here: its λ prices deployment cost only, so it has no counterpart to this project's δ·Risk, which measures detectability exposure. Neither objective has a false-positive term; this project handles false positives in Filter 1's malice-fidelity criterion (D26).

327. **Method note — forward-citation chasing is a distinct search channel.** #326 surfaced because it *cites* #323, not because it matched any keyword query used across ten iterations. Ten passes of keyword search missed it; one reference-verification lookup found it. Keyword search finds papers that describe themselves the way you describe your problem; citation chasing finds papers that *build on* work you already know is relevant, regardless of vocabulary. Both directions matter: backward (a paper's own reference list) and forward (papers citing it). **Action for A17:** run forward-citation searches on the five anchor papers — #305/#312 (Jay), #323 (Osman), Zambianco, Kulkarni, and #326 itself — in Scopus or Google Scholar's "Cited by", not only keyword queries. This is also a known weakness of the review as conducted, and worth one sentence in the review-methodology section of Chapter 2.

328. **MITRE Engage — ATT&CK for ICS mappings** (released 2024; Engage team: Hoffmann, Raymond, Holt, Rodriguez). Maps ICS ATT&CK techniques to adversary-engagement activities, viewable through the Engage Matrix Explorer with raw data on GitHub. ⚠️ **Found at iteration 11 and it cost a claim.** `formal-problem-definition.md` §4 previously asserted this project was "the first to apply [the plausibility rubric] to ICS ATT&CK." Engage is not the same instrument — it maps techniques to engagement activities rather than scoring per-technique decoy feasibility with malice fidelity — but the distinction is definitional, and an examiner aware of Engage would force that argument in a viva. Claim narrowed rather than defended (see §4's revision note). **Engage is now an input, not a threat:** it is a MITRE-curated, citable source for rubric criteria 2 and 3. 🟢 https://medium.com/mitre-engage/releasing-mitre-engage-mappings-for-att-ck-for-ics-and-att-ck-for-mobile-part-1-72bc907dad45 **Where the ICS data actually is** (checked 30 September 2026 in a clone of github.com/mitre/engage at commit 5ae09f6, 1 April 2024): only in `Data/Engage-Data-V1.0.xlsx`, sheet "ICS ATT&CK Mappings" (371 rows, header included). **Negative result:** none of the 11 JSON files under `Data/json/` contains an ICS technique ID (T0xxx), so the JSON need not be searched again.

329. **Refinement to #40 (Valeros et al., "Decoys Cannot Go Everywhere")** *(corrected 1 October 2026: this entry said #53, which is an unrelated IEC 62443 paper; the rubric's source is #40)* **— the Sweep–Seek rule.** Full-text detail not captured on first logging. The paper applies its rubric to all 250 techniques of ATT&CK v18.1 and finds only 80 (32%) admit a decoy the attacker could plausibly reach; for the other 170 there is no defender-controllable asset in the attacker's path. More usefully, decoy encounters across those 80 fall into exactly two patterns: **Sweep** (attacker moves broadly through assets in range, meets the decoy incidentally) and **Seek** (attacker looks for a specific asset type and interacts with a fabricated instance). The paper states this as a rule replacing ad-hoc placement: a decoy must lie on a sweep path or imitate a sought asset, or it goes untouched. ⭐ This is a sharper operational form of rubric criterion 2 than the original logging captured, and it maps onto this project's own path set — P3 is a sweep, P1 is a seek. Folded into `formal-problem-definition.md` §4. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/html/2606.27966

330. **Sæther, Sørensen et al. (2026)** — "Security Aspects of Zones and Conduits in IEC 62443," *Journal of Cybersecurity and Privacy* (MDPI), vol. 6, no. 2, art. 52, DOI 10.3390/jcp6020052. Author list to be confirmed. Addresses SL allocation for **conduits specifically**, proposing that a conduit configuration zone be designated with the highest SL of all conduits it manages, and treating conduits as having two responsibilities — dropping unintended inter-zone connections, and protecting the availability/integrity/confidentiality of permitted ones. ⭐⭐ **Fills a real hole in this project's own model.** `formal-problem-definition.md` §1 defines conduits structurally but assigns them no security level; `Crit(v)` draws SL from `zone(v)` only. Conduits are the structure this project claims as a distinguishing input, yet they currently carry no SL and contribute nothing quantitative. This paper is a standards-grounded, citable rule for closing that. **Decision required before Phase D** — see `iteration-11-findings.md` §4. 🟢 https://www.mdpi.com/2624-800X/6/2/52

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢 full text read; authors corrected** (PDF supplied by you). The author list above is wrong. The authors are Martin Gilje Jaatun, Mary Ann Lundteigen, Christoph Thieme, Lars Halvdan Flå, Karin Bernsmed, Roald Lygre and Fredrik Gratte. The paper concludes that an SL for a conduit is "less useful, but to the extent that it should be used, it should be based on the SL of the highest connecting zones", which verifies D20's rule. It holds that a conduit should run between exactly two zones, and that the network devices realising conduits form a configuration zone given the highest SL of all connected zones, which supports recording the missing OT DMZ–Control conduit (A38). It also argues against giving the high-criticality embedded devices at the bottom of the Purdue model high SL targets, because the zones above protect them. That bears on A37: the `src/graph_model` comment calling a level-rising SL placeholder "IEC 62443's usual convention" is not supported by this source.

331. **Negative result, iteration 11.** Direct vocabulary-inversion search on the exact combination this project claims — IEC 62443 zones and conduits, decoy placement, optimization objective, restricted to recent work — returned only standards explainers, vendor guidance, and the already-logged Zambianco microservices paper. No OT-structured placement optimizer surfaced. Eleven iterations, still nothing occupying the specific cell. Logged as a negative result because negative results are evidence and unlogged searches get repeated.

332. **Shodan Honeyscore** — proprietary honeypot-likelihood score returning a probability 0.0–1.0 for a given IP. Precision measured at **70.3%** on ICS honeypots at a 0.5 threshold, per an independent study reported in Rowe & collaborators' deception-in-depth paper (#337). ⭐ **Existence proof that per-position decoy detectability is already a scalar quantity, externally computable.** Directly relevant to Filter 2, which `formal-problem-definition.md` §4 and `04-deployment-requirements.md` §7 had treated as irreducibly qualitative. 🟢 (secondary-source figure; the Honeyscore algorithm itself is proprietary and unpublished)

333. **(2026)** — "A Review of Honeypots: Fingerprinting Techniques, Detection, and Evasion Mechanisms," *Future Internet* (MDPI), vol. 18, no. 4, art. 190, DOI 10.3390/fi18040190. Author list to be confirmed. ⭐⭐ Systematic review supplying a **taxonomy of fingerprinting artifacts** (static banners, default configurations, timing/latency, handshake/negotiation, partial protocol implementation) crossed with **probing techniques** (single-stage active P1, multi-stage active P2). Central finding: fingerprinting difficulty scales with honeypot interaction level, and low-interaction deployments — the overwhelming majority of ICS/OT honeypots — are identified through surface-level artifacts using basic active probing. **This is the structured basis on which Filter 2 can be made a scoring rubric rather than a judgement call.** Full text not yet read; read before building the rubric. 🟡 https://www.mdpi.com/1999-5903/18/4/190

334. **HoneyJudge** — "A PLC Honeypot Identification Framework Based on Device Memory Testing," IEEE. Models PLC internal memory at system, user and process levels, extracts six memory features via automated network requests, and combines them by weighted voting. Explicitly goes beyond Nmap and PLCScan. Relevant as evidence that fingerprinting capability against ICS decoys is advancing, not static. 🟢 https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10542162/

335. **Vulnerability Assessment of Industrial Control System with an Improved CVSS** (arXiv 2306.08631) and **Dynamic Vulnerability Criticality Calculator for Industrial Control Systems** (arXiv 2404.16854). Both score *vulnerability* severity for ICS assets, adjusting CVSS for OT context. **Deliberately not adopted.** Same lineage TrapManager (#326) uses for node weighting; CVE severity measures exploitability, not operational importance, and this project's `Crit(v)` is built on required protection level and process consequence instead. Logged so the choice is visible as a choice. 🟢

336. **Asset Criticality Analysis (ACA) — established reliability-engineering practice.** Standard formulation: Criticality = Consequence × Likelihood, with Consequence a weighted sum of severity scores across safety, environmental, production, quality, maintenance-cost and customer-impact categories. Some methodologies add a third multiplier, *detectability*. ⭐ **Grounds `Damage(v)`**, which `formal-problem-definition.md` §2 currently defines loosely and elicits ad hoc. Requesting the number in the form a plant's existing ACA already produces largely dissolves the elicitation problem flagged in `04-deployment-requirements.md` §1. **Two cautions:** (i) import the consequence decomposition only — ACA's likelihood term is probability of *failure*, unrelated to probability of *compromise*; (ii) ⚠️ **terminology collision** — ACA "detectability" means whether an impending failure can be detected in advance, the opposite direction from this project's meaning (whether an attacker can identify a decoy). Glossary disambiguation required; see A26. 🟢 Industry practice, multiple concordant secondary sources; cite a primary reliability-engineering text rather than a vendor guide.

337. **Rowe et al. (2024)** — "WiP: Deception-in-Depth Using Multiple Layers of Deception," arXiv:2412.16430. Distinct from the 2025 Springer chapter already logged. Source of the Honeyscore precision figure in #332 and of a survey of honeypot/honeytoken fingerprinting by default configuration, instrumentation artifacts and missing features. Consistent with #333's taxonomy. 🟢 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.16430

---

## Iteration 13 — Software projects, commercial products, and a second academic sweep

First iteration to search *products and code repositories* rather than literature. Prior iterations searched vendors for published methods; this one searched for what vendors and open-source projects actually ship.

338. **Acalvio ShadowPlex — "AI-Enabled Deception Recommendation Engine."** ⚠️⚠️ **The sharpest challenge to the novelty claim found in thirteen iterations, and it is commercial, not academic.** Acalvio's own marketing states the platform "automates network discovery, deception recommendation, and placement" across IT, OT and cloud, and that "deployment is AI-driven and automated… the platform continuously manages deception placement and maintenance."

**Read closely before panicking — what the engine actually recommends is decoy *attributes*, not network *positions*.** Acalvio's own description: the engine "generates specific properties that enable deceptions to blend into the production network. For decoys, the engine recommends properties like hostnames, operating system versions, and services… the 100+ AD attributes for honeytoken accounts." That is *realism* recommendation — what a decoy should look like so it blends into a given subnet — which is the deception-*generation* problem this project explicitly does not address. The "placement" language refers to automated provisioning and lifecycle management across discovered subnets, not selection of a subset of positions maximising a stated objective.

**Four distinctions, and they must be stated in section 14 rather than left implicit:** (i) no published objective function — nothing is stated to be maximised; (ii) no published baseline comparison — no evidence any positioning outperforms an alternative; (iii) not reproducible — proprietary and unpublished, so no formulation exists to compare against; (iv) no IEC 62443 zone or conduit structure appears anywhere in the public material. **Consequence for the claim: the word "published" in the novelty statement is load-bearing and must never be dropped.** The defensible claim is about the published literature, not about what proprietary products may do internally. 🟢 https://www.acalvio.com/resources/guide/acalvio-cyber-deception-buyers-guide/

339. **Commvault Threatwise (formerly TrapX DeceptionGrid)** — described in third-party surveys as offering "AI-guided decoy placement" across IT, OT and backup environments. Same assessment as #338: a product capability, no published method. **FortiDeceptor** similarly "automatically discovers network resources and recommends suitable decoys" — discovery-driven suggestion, not objective-driven optimisation. Logged so the commercial landscape is on record as checked at product level, not only at publication level. 🟢

340. **Open-source landscape — searched directly for the first time.** DejaVu (multi-VLAN decoy deployment and management console), Beelzebub (LLM-powered decoy services), DecoyNet (deception plus ML behaviour analysis), OpenCanary, Cowrie, Conpot, T-Pot, Honeytrap, OWASP Python-Honeypot. **Every one is generation, deployment or management. None performs placement optimisation.** DejaVu is the closest in spirit — it deploys decoys "strategically across their network on different VLANs" — but placement is an administrator's manual choice through a console. 🟢

341. **UHBS — Universal Honeypot Benchmarking Standard** (Apache-2.0, `pip install uhbs`). Vendor-neutral 0–100 scoring of decoy realism, containment, scale and telemetry quality. Not placement, but relevant to the detectability filter: an independent, quantitative measure of decoy *quality* exists, which is the complement of what Filter 2 estimates. Possible future cross-reference for Filter 2's scoring rubric (A23). 🟢 https://pypi.org/project/uhbs/

342. **"Time-to-Lie: Identifying Industrial Control System Honeypots Using the Internet Control Message Protocol"** (arXiv 2410.17731). ICMP/TTL-based fingerprinting of ICS honeypots specifically. Reinforces #333 and the ICSLure evidence: ICS decoys are identifiable through surface-level network artifacts. Directly supports Filter 2's existence and the A23 scoring rubric. 🟢

343. **Two further IT-domain placement papers, both two-term objectives.** "Honeypot Allocation for Cyber Deception in Dynamic Tactical Networks" (arXiv 2308.11817) — game-theoretic, maximises hit likelihood minus deception and reconfiguration cost, for mobile tactical networks. "Anti-Honeypot Enabled Optimal Attack Strategy for Industrial Cyber-Physical Systems" (IEEE 2020) — attacker-side, derives an optimal strategy for defeating honeypot-protected ICPS, which is the mirror image of this project's problem and useful threat-model support for Filter 2. Neither is a competitor; both reinforce the two-term-objective pattern noted in #326. 🟢

344. **Brown, Green et al. — "Don't get stung, cover your ICS in honey"** (*Computers & Security*, 2022). Maps ICS honeypots onto the UK Cyber Assessment Framework and NIST objectives, and gives placement *advice* — deploy in an isolated range, avoid high-interaction honeypots inside the operational network. Qualitative guidance, explicitly not optimisation. A useful citation for "placement in OT is currently advisory" in section 14. 🟢 https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0167404821004211

345. **Negative result, iteration 13.** A second academic sweep on optimal honeypot placement in ICS with Purdue/zone structure and attack-path coverage returned no new competitor. Thirteen iterations, still nothing occupying the cell.

346. **Incidental observation, worth recording.** `github.com/nittsOG/idecop-ics` is now the **top result** for "github open source tool deception decoy placement optimization ICS OT network." Two consequences: the repository is publicly discoverable and indexed, which establishes a visible timestamp on the work; and any future iteration searching this vocabulary will surface this project itself, so searches must be phrased to exclude it or the result will read as a false confirmation.

347. **US 9,853,999 B2 — "Context-aware knowledge system and methods for deploying deception mechanisms."** Acalvio Technologies; Singh, Neema, Desai, Sama, Gopalakrishna. Provisional 62/328,224 (27 Apr 2016), filed 3 Feb 2017, granted 26 Dec 2017; publication US 2017/0318053. ⚠️⚠️⚠️ **The closest commercial prior art found, and it is a genuine placement method — not attribute recommendation.** A "deception profiler" selects a subnetwork by highest **asset density** or by an **importance score** (densities plus asset types), computes a **critical-asset density** (critical assets in a portion ÷ critical assets site-wide) and a **summary statistic over historical attack counts**, and from these determines **how many** deception mechanisms to deploy there. **The output is a count for a subnet, not a set of positions.** No objective function, no budget constraint, no interception-stage reasoning, no zone/conduit structure, no baseline validation. **Corrects iteration 13's conclusion**, which inferred from marketing material that Acalvio recommends only decoy attributes — true of the Recommendation Engine as marketed, false of the patent estate. Full comparison in `03-commercial-prior-art-analysis.md`. Granted claims not yet read; abstract-and-description level only. 🟡 https://patents.justia.com/patent/20170318053

    **Update, 6 October 2026 — 🟢 granted claims read** (the granted patent, and a Justia printout of publication US 2017/0318053 A1, supplied by you). Independent claims 1, 19 and 20: identify a network; compute asset densities from the real-time total of assets; compute a summary statistic of historical attacks and derive an attack probability; dynamically determine the number of deception mechanisms, updated in real time; deploy them. Dependent claims add density ratios (3–4), an importance score (5) and deployment "on or near the central node" (6); in FIG. 13's example that is the node with the most incoming connections, which an attacker is likely to go through. Claims 10–14 cover configurations, 15–17 schedules and 18 SOC/CSIRT/IDS/IPS integration; the description also gives a weighted count per subnet. **Verdict: the novelty claim holds at method level.** The patent combines a count rule with a single central-node rule, while this project selects a set of positions by maximising a stated objective under a budget. "The output is a count for a subnet" above is incomplete, since claim 6 also fixes where. The centrality baseline is the nearest analogue of the patented rule. Closes A29's claims half.

    **Update, 15 November 2026 — the sweep (D36).** Google Patents lists 21 Acalvio patents (run P2a, #627), all related (T1) on their titles; one other page, US 10,326,796 B1 (#918), was opened. The patents that cite this one were listed and screened (run P4, #638): none of the nine opened chooses decoy positions by maximising an objective. D35's reading of the claims stands.

348. **US 12,375,527 — "Leveraging network security scanning to obtain enhanced information regarding an attack chain involving a decoy file."** Incidental find. Its deception-host deployment module "may also recommend suitable deception hosts and its appropriate placement in the private network **based on historical knowledge of the attack and attack types**." Same family of idea as #347 — threat-history-driven placement suggestion, no objective, no optimisation. Logged as evidence that threat-informed placement recommendation recurs across the patent literature. 🟡 https://image-ppubs.uspto.gov/dirsearch-public/print/downloadPdf/12375527

349. **Acalvio patent estate, context.** Over 25 granted US patents. Others identified but not analysed: US 10,616,276 / US 2017/0310706 "Tunneling for network deceptions" (the Deception Farms projection architecture — deployment mechanics, not placement selection); US 10,348,763 "Responsive deception mechanisms"; US 9,985,988 "Deception to detect network scans". Marketing also claims "creates unique, non-fingerprintable decoys" — a generation-side detectability claim, the complement of this project's Filter 2, and worth revisiting if Filter 2's rubric (A23) needs an adversary-capability baseline. **A systematic patent sweep across Acalvio, Commvault/TrapX, Fortinet, SentinelOne/Attivo and Proofpoint/Illusive is now a real gap — comparable in importance to A17 and, unlike A17, requiring no institutional credentials.** 🟢

350. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — T0811, Data from Information Repositories.** Verified directly at attack.mitre.org/techniques/T0811/. Collection tactic. **Targeted Assets list names A0006 Data Historian and Engineering Workstation explicitly**, which is what grounds P4's step sequence rather than a plausible reconstruction. Procedure examples include Dragonfly 2.0 (accessed workstations and servers holding ICS reference documents — wiring diagrams, panel layouts, vendor names) and Duqu (S0038). Also cites a 2011–2013 campaign against oil and gas organisations that searched document repositories for system manuals, RTU site lists and remote dial-up access information. 🟢 https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T0811/
    **Update, 2 November 2026 — 🟢 corrected against the v17.1 and live pages.** Two claims above are wrong. The ATT&CK v17.1 page (supplied by you on 31 October 2026) and the live page, read in the independent reviews (D32), list A0006 Data Historian and A0007 as T0811's targeted assets, and no engineering workstation. The only procedure example is Duqu (S0038), not Dragonfly 2.0. P4's Historian target and step 2 stand; its step 3, T0811 at the Engineering WS, loses its listed-target grounding (D33 §7). D21's wording is corrected in the next documentation pass.

351. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — T0862, Supply Chain Compromise.** Dragonfly's documented initial-access vector: legitimate ICS vendor software trojanised at source. Verified against MITRE-authored ATT&CK for ICS material (Otis Alexander, ATT&CK for ICS lead — technique-sequence material showing OEM website → workstation → controller). 🟡 verified from MITRE-authored secondary material; the live technique page's Dragonfly procedure-example entry was not opened. Confirm before the Implementation chapter cites it as a procedure example rather than as a technique.

352. **Chaudhry, Andersen, Choudhary & Dragoni (2026)** — "A Review of Honeypots: Fingerprinting Techniques, Detection, and Evasion Mechanisms," *Future Internet*, vol. 18, no. 4, art. 190, DOI 10.3390/fi18040190. DTU, Department of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science. Completes the bibliographic record for #333. Categorises fingerprinting techniques **T1–T7**, susceptibility artifacts **A1–A8** and probing techniques, and finds that fingerprinting techniques and susceptibility artifacts scale in complexity alongside honeypot interaction level — low-interaction deployments, which ICS decoys overwhelmingly are, fall to surface-level artifacts under basic active probing. Supplies the structural framework for the Filter 2 rubric (D22). 🟢 **Full text read (D22a).** Explicitly coded in the text: A1 static banners, A4 incomplete state machines, A5 virtualisation artifacts, A7 default configurations, A8 behavioural inconsistencies; A2/A3/A6 and T6 appear only in figures. Probing techniques all named: P1 single-stage active, P2 multi-stage active (most prevalent), P3 malformed/fuzzing, P4 timing, P5 differential, P6 cross-protocol, P7 longitudinal. ⭐⭐ **Contains independent support for this project's premise:** measured evidence that corporate-network honeypots received 91% more S7comm traffic than cloud-hosted ones while cloud saw 45% more HTTP, authors concluding "setting and placement are critical"; and for smart grids, that deployment complexity "can help mask the suspiciousness of the honeypot's vulnerability." Cite in section 14. ~~The full A1–A8 artifact list was not obtained~~ — the rubric was therefore built from individually verified artifact evidence across #332, #334, #342 and the ICSLure evaluation, using this paper's structural principle rather than reproducing its codes. Obtain the full text before the Methodology chapter cites specific artifact codes. https://doi.org/10.3390/fi18040190 *Re-read 26 October 2026 for D31, from the full text at https://www.mdpi.com/1999-5903/18/4/190, to score five new candidates.* Used: §4.9 (ICS/OT decoys identified by static banners and default configurations under basic active probing); §5.2 (a single Modbus request, or a single scan, separates PLC honeypots; a single SSH connection yields a HASSH fingerprint); §4.3 and §6.2 (SSH version strings and default OpenSSH versions); §4.4 and §6.1 (web endpoint checks and default TLS certificates). **Negative result, logged so the search is not repeated:** the text does not discuss firewall, router or switch honeypots (its closest mention is static emulation of CPE devices in IoT, §4.2), and it names neither RTUs nor IEC-104. The definitions of P1–P7 and A1–A8 appear only in its figures.

### NN. The approximation-guarantee primary source (closes A27)

353. **Harshaw, Feldman, Ward & Karbasi (2019)** — "Submodular Maximization beyond Non-negativity: Guarantees, Fast Algorithms, and Applications," *Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning*, PMLR vol. 97, pp. 2634–2643. arXiv:1904.09354. 🟢 **Full text read (D23); citation cross-checked against the published PMLR record.** ⭐⭐ **This is the source that closes A27 and licenses the only approximation guarantee this project claims.** Addresses exactly our objective's form, **f = g − c** with g monotone, non-negative and γ-weakly submodular and c non-negative modular. **Distorted Greedy** returns S with **g(S) − c(S) ≥ (1 − e^−γ)·g(OPT) − c(OPT)** using O(nk) evaluations of g; the bound for Distorted Greedy is stated deterministically, while the *stochastic* variants carry the analogous bound in expectation with an additional −ε term. Our g is genuinely submodular rather than merely weakly submodular, so **γ = 1** and the bound is (1 − 1/e) ≈ 0.632; the preconditions are verified on the frozen instance by `scripts/structure_check.py`, which tests the aggregate g and c directly and tests c for **modularity**, not merely submodularity. The paper additionally proves a **matching hardness result** — no polynomial-time algorithm accessing g through a value oracle can do better than (1 − e^−γ) — which is our access model, so the bound is the best obtainable and not merely one we happen to hold. Appendix A, *"Greedy Performs Arbitrarily Poorly"*, constructs an instance on which **standard greedy's ratio is unbounded**; the failure mode is a high-gain/high-cost "bad element" that, once taken, drives every remaining marginal gain below its cost. ⚠️ **Verification boundary, stated because two earlier claims of mine about this paper were wrong** (see D23): the form, the bound, γ = 1, O(nk), the determinism of the Distorted Greedy bound, the hardness result, the appendix title and the unbounded-ratio conclusion are all verified; the **theorem's number is unconfirmed** — one retrieval route reported Theorem 1, a second could not corroborate it — and a previously asserted **O(1/k)** rate for plain greedy is **not** in the text. The result is therefore cited by content, never by theorem number, and never as O(1/k). https://proceedings.mlr.press/v97/harshaw19a.html

354. **Sviridenko, Vondrák & Ward — the regularised g − ℓ result.** 🟡 **Named but not obtained; route rejected on practicality grounds, not on correctness.** `formal-problem-definition.md` §7(i) originally named this as the route to a guarantee, and A27 was opened to verify it. It was superseded rather than verified: their bound is comparable, but their algorithm requires continuous optimisation of the **multilinear extension**, which is impractical at any scale and disproportionate at this one, whereas #353's algorithm is combinatorial and about fifteen lines. ⚠️ **This characterisation comes from #353's discussion of related work, not from Sviridenko et al.'s own text, which was never obtained.** It is adequate for rejecting the route — the claim being relied on is only "impractical", and the project cites #353's bound rather than theirs — but if the thesis states anything about *their* theorem specifically, the paper must be obtained first. Logged so this is not silently upgraded to a verified claim later.

355. **Negative result, D23.** No source was sought or found for transferring an approximation guarantee to **plain** greedy on a non-monotone objective, because #353's Appendix A rules it out directly. Recorded so the question is not reopened: plain greedy on F(x) has **no** approximation guarantee, F(x) being submodular but not monotone, and the distorted-greedy bound must not be transferred to it.

### OO. Licensing of the Jay (2023) PDF — for A18

356. **IEEE Open — Frequently Asked Questions (open-access licensing).** 🟢 **Verified directly (D24 session).** States that *"All open access articles are published under either the Creative Commons Attribution License (CC-BY) or the Creative Commons Attribution, NonCommercial, No Derivatives License (CC BY-NC-ND)"*. Under CC BY, users may distribute, reuse, modify and build upon the work with attribution; under CC BY-NC-ND, reuse is permitted with credit to the author, provided it is not commercial and does not change the work. Both therefore permit non-commercial redistribution of an **unmodified** article with attribution. This is the basis for recommending that A18 be closed without rewriting the repository's history: #305/#312 (Jay, 2023) is an *IEEE Access* article, and *IEEE Access* is fully open access. Licence reading, not legal advice. https://open.ieee.org/about/faqs/

That settles the question this entry left open, and A18's recommendation stands unchanged. *Original entry, superseded:* ⚠️ Needs another access route. The IEEE Xplore article page (https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10132467/) is rendered by JavaScript and returned no content through the fetch tool. The DOAJ record (https://doaj.org/article/f0c53713399849328f420e9e3b03d2bd) confirms the article is indexed as open access but its retrieved text carried no licence statement. The DOAJ journal record for *IEEE Access* could not be fetched in-session. ### PP. Build environment

358. **PyPI — OR-Tools binary wheels by Python version.** 🟢 **Verified directly (28 September 2026)** by requesting manylinux x86_64 binary wheels from PyPI for each Python version: `ortools 9.15.6755` publishes wheels for **CPython 3.11, 3.12, 3.13 and 3.14**. Checked because the user's Kali machine may run a newer Python than the development environment; it means `pip install -r requirements.txt` (`ortools>=9.9`) needs no source build on any of those versions. `04-deployment-requirements.md` says only "Python 3.x", which this does not contradict. Logged because it was cited in a chat reply, under the rule that every source cited anywhere is recorded — so the check is not silently repeated. *Re-checked 13 October 2026* against PyPI's file list for `ortools 9.15.6755`, still the latest release. Wheels exist for CPython 3.9 to 3.14 on Windows (x86-64 only), macOS (arm64 and x86-64) and Linux (x86-64 and aarch64). There is none for 3.15 and no source distribution. So on Python 3.15, `pip install -r requirements.txt` fails with "No matching distribution found for ortools>=9.9" (reproduced on 3.15.0b4). The install succeeds on 3.10 to 3.14, and the legacy sweep prints identical output on each (Linux). `04-deployment-requirements.md` now states this range. ### QQ. Pre-annotation and anchoring evidence — for A32's scoring route

359. **Fort & Sagot (2010)** — "Influence of Pre-annotation on POS-tagged Corpus Development," Fourth Linguistic Annotation Workshop (LAW IV), ACL Anthology W10-1807. 🟢 PDF read through the fetch tool. Controlled experiments on part-of-speech annotation with and without automatic pre-annotation. Pre-annotation "allows for a gain in quality" but introduces biases "that should be identified and notified to the annotators, so that they can be extra careful during correction"; "the rather good results of the taggers cause the attention of the annotators to be reduced". https://aclanthology.org/W10-1807/

360. **Lingren et al. (2014)** — "Evaluating the impact of pre-annotation on annotation speed and potential bias: natural language processing gold standard development for clinical named entity recognition in clinical trial announcements," *JAMIA* 21(3):406–413. 🟢 Abstract read on the OUP article page; the JAMIA PDF route returned HTTP 400 on 30 September. Dictionary-based pre-annotation: "Time savings ranged from 13.85% to 21.5% per entity"; "There was no statistically significant difference for IAA and annotator performance in pre-annotations." https://academic.oup.com/jamia/article/21/3/406/2909253

361. **Mikulová, Straka, Štěpánek, Štěpánková, Hajič (2022)** — "Quality and Efficiency of Manual Annotation: Pre-annotation Bias," LREC 2022 (ACL Anthology 2022.lrec-1.312; arXiv:2306.09307). 🟢 Abstract read. Dependency-syntax annotation: pre-annotation gave faster manual annotation and "increases the consistency of the resulting annotation" without reducing its quality. https://aclanthology.org/2022.lrec-1.312/

362. **Schroeder, Roy, Kabbara (2025)** — "Just Put a Human in the Loop? Investigating LLM-Assisted Annotation for Subjective Tasks," Findings of ACL 2025 (2025.findings-acl.1323); arXiv:2507.15821. 🟢 Full text read via the arXiv HTML through the fetch tool. 410 annotators and over 7,000 annotations on two interpretive codebooks, with suggestions shown after the annotator had read the text. Annotators "strongly took the LLM suggestions, significantly changing the label distribution compared to the baseline". Overlap between crowd labels and GPT's suggested labels was 81–87% with suggestions, against about 40% without. Using the assisted labels as ground truth raised GPT's measured F1 from .47 to .79. Suggestions raised self-reported confidence but did not make annotators faster. The authors conclude that "simply reviewing LLM suggestions will nudge the distribution of label outputs towards an LLM baseline, even if humans are given a chance to review the outputs." ⭐ The closest evidence found to D12's process: an LLM first pass on an interpretive rubric, then human review. https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15821

363. **Beck, Eckman, Kern, Kreuter (2026)** — "Bias in the Loop: How Humans Evaluate AI-Generated Suggestions," *Harvard Data Science Review* 8(2), Spring 2026; arXiv:2509.08514. 🟢 Read via the arXiv HTML through the fetch tool; the HDSR page returned HTTP 403 on 30 September. 2,784 participants reviewed AI pre-annotations (greenhouse-gas figures extracted from corporate tables) in a 2×2×2 factorial design. Requiring a correction whenever a suggestion was flagged wrong "resulted in significantly fewer corrections" and more acceptance of incorrect suggestions. Attitude to AI was the strongest predictor: sceptical reviewers "detected errors more reliably", favourable ones showed "dangerous overreliance". Relevant because overriding a score in Screen 3 means choosing the replacement value. https://hdsr.mitpress.mit.edu/pub/nrcn4h7d

### UU. Network devices as ICS attack targets — for the backup-conduit question (4 October 2026)

377. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — Assets.** 🟢 Fetched 4 October 2026. The list has eighteen assets, A0001–A0018. Network infrastructure is among them: **A0014 Routers**, **A0015 Switch**, **A0016 Firewall**. Relevant because the testbed's Backup Control Switch and OT Firewall are asset types that ATT&CK for ICS itself treats as attack targets. https://attack.mitre.org/assets/

378. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — A0015 Switch.** 🟢 Fetched 4 October 2026. "A network device that connects endpoints (e.g., workstations, servers, HMIs, PLCs, etc.) so that they can communicate and share data and resources." It lists 33 techniques that target switches, among them T0830 Adversary-in-the-Middle, T0842 Network Sniffing, T0866 Exploitation of Remote Services, T0859 Valid Accounts, T0886 Remote Services, T1695 Block Communications and T1693 Modify Firmware. https://attack.mitre.org/assets/A0015/

379. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — T0830 Adversary-in-the-Middle.** 🟢 Fetched 4 October 2026. Collection tactic: "Adversaries with privileged network access may seek to modify network traffic in real time using adversary-in-the-middle (AiTM) attacks." Its targeted assets include Switch and Routers. Its procedure examples are TEMP.Veles and VPNFilter (#380). https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T0830/

380. **MITRE ATT&CK — S1010 VPNFilter.** 🟢 Fetched 4 October 2026. A "multi-stage, modular platform" that infected network devices. It maps to two ICS techniques:
    - T0830 Adversary-in-the-Middle: the ssler module;
    - T0842 Network Sniffing: a packet-sniffer module "monitors ICS traffic", looking for credentials in Modbus communications on TP-Link R600-VPN devices.

    MITRE's references are Talos (Largent 2018; Hurd 2019), not read here. This is documented adversary behaviour against network infrastructure carrying ICS traffic. https://attack.mitre.org/software/S1010/

381. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — T0842 Network Sniffing.** 🟢 Fetched 4 October 2026.
    - *Tactic:* Discovery (TA0102).
    - *Procedure examples:* INCONTROLLER (tcpdump), Stuxnet (a replaced Profibus receive block) and VPNFilter ("monitors ICS traffic").
    - *Targeted assets:* include Switch, Jump Host and PLC.

    Grounds P5's sniffing step at the Backup Control Switch. https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T0842/

382. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — T0866 Exploitation of Remote Services.** 🟢 Fetched 4 October 2026.
    - *Tactics:* Initial Access and Lateral Movement.
    - *Procedure examples:* Bad Rabbit, NotPetya and WannaCry spreading to industrial networks through MS17-010, and Stuxnet propagating through WinCC SQL.
    - *Targeted assets:* include Switch, Routers, Jump Host and PLC.

    Grounds P5's step onto the switch. https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T0866/

383. **MITRE ATT&CK for ICS — T0822 External Remote Services.** 🟢 Fetched 4 October 2026.
    - *Tactic:* Initial Access.
    - *Procedure examples:* in the 2015 Ukraine attack, Sandworm "utilized valid credentials … to access the control system VPN used by grid operators"; in the 2025 Poland wiper attacks, initial access came through compromised Fortinet edge devices; also Fuxnet and the Maroochy water breach.
    - *Targeted assets:* include Jump Host, Switch, Routers, Firewall and VPN Server.

    Grounds P5's entry at the DMZ Jump Host. https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T0822/

### XX. The development environment — for D29 (14 October 2026)

391. **nodejs-wheel and nodejs-wheel-binaries — Node.js packaged as Python wheels (PyPI).** 🟢 Read on PyPI on 14 October 2026: the project description and the file list.
- **What it is.** It describes itself as "an unofficial repository to distribute Node.js prebuilt wheels through PyPI". The maintainer is Jinzhe Zeng, the licence is MIT, and it is built with scikit-build-core and cibuildwheel.
- **Commands.** `node`, `npm`, `npx` and `corepack` come only with `nodejs-wheel`. `nodejs-wheel-binaries` is its dependency.
- **Platforms.** Version 24.19.0 (uploaded 19 August 2026) has wheels for Windows (x64 and ARM64), macOS 13.5 or later (Intel and Apple silicon), and Linux with glibc 2.28 or later (x86-64 and aarch64).
- **Adoption.** basedpyright 1.40.2 declares `nodejs-wheel-binaries>=20.13.1` as a dependency.
- 🟡 **Not established:** whether the Node binaries are compiled in its own CI or repackaged from nodejs.org. A fetch of the repository README from GitHub returned nothing.

Cited by D29 and `04-setup-guide.md`. https://pypi.org/project/nodejs-wheel/ · https://github.com/njzjz/nodejs-wheel

392. **uv — its README on PyPI (Astral).** 🟢 Read on 14 October 2026, at version 0.13.1, uploaded that day (MIT or Apache-2.0).
- **Installation.**
  - macOS and Linux: `curl -LsSf https://astral.sh/uv/install.sh | sh`.
  - Windows: `powershell -ExecutionPolicy ByPass -c "irm https://astral.sh/uv/install.ps1 | iex"`.
  - Either: `pip install uv` or `pipx install uv`.
- **Documented features this project uses:**
  - `uv python pin` writes `.python-version`;
  - `uv venv` downloads a Python version as needed;
  - `uv pip compile --universal` writes a platform-independent requirements file;
  - `uv pip sync` installs one.
- **Also tested directly with 0.13.1 (D29).**
  - Outside a project, `uv run` uses the `.venv` it finds in the current or a parent folder. With no `.venv`, it uses the computer's own Python.
  - `uv venv` refuses to overwrite an existing `.venv` unless given `--clear`.
  - `uv pip sync` stops on a checksum mismatch. With `--dry-run` it prints "Would make no changes" when the environment already matches.
  - `uv run` on a script carrying inline metadata (a `# /// script` block) runs it in a separate temporary environment, and passes the path of uv in the `UV` variable. `scripts/setup.py` relies on both.

https://pypi.org/project/uv/ · https://docs.astral.sh/uv/

394. **Rethlefsen M.L. et al., PRISMA-S Group (2021)** — "PRISMA-S: an extension to the PRISMA Statement for Reporting Literature Searches in Systematic Reviews," *Systematic Reviews* 10:39, doi:10.1186/s13643-020-01542-z, CC BY 4.0. 🟢 Its Table 1 checklist of 16 items was read verbatim from the journal in the A17 review (30 October 2026, D32). The protocol meets items 1, 5, 8, 9, 13, 15 and 16, and plans item 12's update search. https://doi.org/10.1186/s13643-020-01542-z

395. **Gusenbauer & Haddaway (2020)** — a comparison of academic search systems for systematic reviews, *Research Synthesis Methods*, doi:10.1002/jrsm.1378. 🟡 Abstract only, read through a secondary page in the A17 review: it calls Google Scholar inappropriate as a principal search system. Read the open full text before citing it.

396. **Wohlin (2014)** — guidelines for snowballing (citation chasing) in systematic literature studies, EASE '14, doi:10.1145/2601248.2601268. 🟡 Bibliographic record only: OpenAlex record W1975675278, read as raw JSON in the A17 review (cited by 3,923 there, 30 October 2026). Read it before citing it.

397. **OpenAlex — API documentation.** 🟢 The Searching and Filtering help pages, the LLM quick reference (revised 15 October 2026) and the recipes page, read in the A17 review. Recorded there: `search.title_and_abstract`; uppercase AND, OR and NOT, with parentheses and quoted phrases; stemming on; URLs up to about 4 KB; `filter=cites:` for forward citations; and a key recommended but not required, with a free daily budget of about 100 searches. https://developers.openalex.org

398. **Semantic Scholar — Academic Graph API tutorial.** 🟢 Read in the A17 review. Bulk search takes `+` for AND, `|` for OR and `-` for NOT, with quotes, `*` and parentheses. Its `total` is described as an estimate. A key is optional; keyless users share one pool, so "429 too many requests" replies are possible. https://webflow.semanticscholar.org/product/api/tutorial

399. **arXiv — API basics.** 🟢 Read in the A17 review. Only the base URL and the `all:` prefix were confirmed there; the page does not give the field syntax. https://info.arxiv.org/help/api/basics.html

400. **Crossref — REST API tips.** 🟢 Read in the A17 review. The page does not document boolean search, so whether a Crossref total counts the records matching a string is unconfirmed. The protocol uses Crossref for DOI and metadata lookups only.

### YY. A17 structured search — the kept records (D35, 11 November 2026)

One entry per record kept by A17's screening (`01-search-protocol.md` §10), except the 24 already in this log, which `01-search-screening-record.md` §9 maps to their entries. "Record" numbers are the screening record's, not this log's. Markers: 🟢 full text read; 🟡 judged on its abstract or title only, so verify before citing; 🔴 full text not obtained (the user's free-only rule, D35).

**T2: narrows the claim (1)**

417. **Multi‐Layered Optimization for Adaptive Decoy Placement in Cyber‐Resilient Power Systems Under Uncertain Attack Scenarios** (2025). doi:10.1049/rpg2.70078. A17 record 269: **T2**, judged on its full text. 🟢 Hua Dong, Zhao Wei, Cui Peiyi, Liu Yiqing and Hua Hua, names as printed (Wiley lists the first author as Dong). *IET Renewable Power Generation* 19 (2025), e70078; open access, CC BY. Read in full on 11 November 2026 from the PDF the user supplied. A three-level (defender–attacker–defender) optimisation: binary decoy-node placement under a budget constraint, an objective that combines resilience loss, operating cost and risk, and node weights for load, topology and operational criticality; modified IEEE 123-bus distribution system. Its "zones" are decoy coverage zones, not IEC 62443 zones. No ATT&CK; compared with statistical anomaly detection and "classical optimisation", not with centrality or random placement. No data or code released ("No data are available for this study"). **T2, confirmed by the user on 11 November 2026 (D35);** the clause-by-clause mapping is in `01-search-screening-record.md` §3.

**Tier unresolved: full text not obtained (8)**

418. **Active Defense Simulation Evaluation of Industrial Control Systems Based on Attack-Defense Graph** (2024). doi:10.1007/978-3-031-71467-2_26. A17 record 41: **tier unresolved**, judged on its abstract only (full text not obtained). 🔴 ICS active defence evaluated on an attack-defence graph; simulates honeypot positions, and three honeypots spread across hierarchy levels beat three in one level (+24.4% time-to-compromise). 610. **Negative result: free copies of A17's paywalled full-text candidates (9–10 November 2026).** OpenAlex's open-access record for each of the thirteen full-text candidates, read through Claude's fetch tool, marked six closed with no free location (records 89, 333, 151, 188, 41 and 160) and gave free locations for six (288 hybrid; 269 and the two *IEEE Access* papers gold; 368 through its SSRN preprint; 307 through DiVA). One web search each for free copies of records 89 and 333 found none. The user then obtained four (269, 368, 290, 72) and could not reach 288 or 307. 🟢 as a record of what was searched.

611. **Backward round from Park & Dagher's reference list (§7.4), queued, not screened.** Two of its closest references are already logged: Jay 2023 (#305) and Ngo et al. 2024 (#27). Three are not: Tan et al. 2025, *IEEE Transactions on Information Forensics and Security*, honeypoint defence for power-IoT PLCs with a time-delay evolutionary game; Wang et al. 2025, *Neurocomputing*, deception decisions from Bayesian attack graphs and Stackelberg games; Kamdem et al. 2024, *IEEE Access*, a two-layer deception signalling game for CPS. Named from #192's version of record; 🟡 unread. They are screened with the forward-citation round.

    **Update, 15 November 2026 — screened (D36).** The three references were screened with the forward-citation round (#622). Tan et al. 2025: tier unresolved, abstract only (#646). Wang et al. 2025, "Turn the tables": related (T1), abstract only (#686). Kamdem et al. 2024: related (T1), abstract only (#687).

612. **US Patent 12,335,308, "System and method for modelling a cyber-physical system to act as a honeypot for cyberattacks" — lead for A29.** Surfaced on 10 November 2026 by an unrelated web search; title and USPTO PDF address only (image-ppubs.uspto.gov/dirsearch-public/print/downloadPdf/12335308). 🟡 unread: by its title, it models a CPS to act as a honeypot rather than choosing positions, which A29's sweep should confirm from the claims.

    **Update, 15 November 2026 — read (A29 run P5, #639; D36).** US 12,335,308 B1 (University of Toronto, #927): a virtual copy of a cyber-physical system, with a control-barrier-function safety set, projects whether an attacker's payload would leave the safe set and takes a safety action; microgrid and generation examples. No choice of decoy positions. Related (T1), as its title suggested.

### ZZ. A17 and A29: the remaining searches, the patent arm and what they kept (D36, 15 November 2026)

One entry per run and round (`01-search-protocol.md` §10), per amendment, per kept paper (records 376–562 of `01-search-screening-record.md`, whose numbers are not this log's) and, under protocol amendment 4, per patent whose page was opened. Patents judged on their titles alone appear in the screening record only, and the papers named in the tables of run P6 appear in one grouped entry. Markers for papers: 🟢 full text read; 🟡 judged on an abstract, a list snippet or a title, so verify before citing; 🔴 full text not obtained (the user's free-only rule, D35). Markers for patents: 🟢 title, abstract and the first independent claim read on the Google Patents page; 🟡 abstract read but claims not reached, or judged on a title and list snippet. Chinese and Korean documents were read through Google's machine translation, which often loses formulas, so what is said of them is a reading of that translation. Patent dates are priority, then publication.

**Paper-side searches and rounds**

613. **A17 run 11 — arXiv, S1 (the recency check of protocol §3). Received 14 November 2026.** Endpoint `export.arxiv.org/api/query`, with `start=0&max_results=1000&sortBy=submittedDate&sortOrder=descending`, no date or category limit. Every term of S1 is matched on title and abstract (`ti:` and `abs:`). Query exactly as run, before URL encoding:
```
(ti:honeypot OR abs:honeypot OR ti:honeynet OR abs:honeynet OR ti:honeytoken OR abs:honeytoken OR ti:decoy OR abs:decoy OR ti:"cyber deception" OR abs:"cyber deception" OR ti:"defensive deception" OR abs:"defensive deception" OR ti:"deception technology" OR abs:"deception technology" OR ti:"deceptive defense" OR abs:"deceptive defense") AND (ti:placement OR abs:placement OR ti:allocation OR abs:allocation OR ti:deployment OR abs:deployment OR ti:selection OR abs:selection OR ti:positioning OR abs:positioning) AND (ti:"industrial control" OR abs:"industrial control" OR ti:SCADA OR abs:SCADA OR ti:ICS OR abs:ICS OR ti:"operational technology" OR abs:"operational technology" OR ti:"cyber physical" OR abs:"cyber physical" OR ti:"programmable logic controller" OR abs:"programmable logic controller" OR ti:"smart grid" OR abs:"smart grid" OR ti:substation OR abs:substation OR ti:"critical infrastructure" OR abs:"critical infrastructure" OR ti:IIoT OR abs:IIoT)
```

640. **A29 run P6 — the similarity route (protocol amendment 7, #643). 15 November 2026.** From the Google Patents page of each of nine patents (level 1: CN 121356815 A, CN 122339846 A, CN 121012683 A, CN 117675308 A, US 12,261,855 B2, CN 120034354 A, CN 118694612 B, CN 121603304 B, CN 118842645 A) the rows of four tables were taken: "Similar documents", "Patent citations", "Cited by" and "Families citing this family", patents and papers alike. Seven pages that level 1 led to (level 2: CN 119254537 A, CN 121664448 A, CN 117240502 A, EP 4 586 550 A1, CN 120896776 B, CN 119182584 B, CN 120498898 A) were read the same way, and the route stopped there. The pages were read through Claude's fetch tool, which returns a small model's summary, so a row can be wrong; the titles are those Google Patents shows.
- **Why:** CN 121356815 A (#771) came up in run P1b among patents the title rule had excluded. It is a placement method with a stated multi-objective function that no keyword run could have returned: Google's list cut its title, and its text has no word for an industrial or power setting. The tables are Google's own similarity and citation links and do not depend on our query words.
- **Patent rows: 243** (131 on the level-1 pages, 112 on the level-2 pages), **229 distinct patents.** The two kinds of one patent are counted once, and a row was matched to the data without regard to its kind code, so a row for CN 119182584 B counts as the patent already held as CN 119182584 A.
  - **60 carry a deception term in the title** (the rule of `01-search-screening-record.md` §14.1). 27 were in the data already: 13 read earlier, 13 related (T1) on a title only (2 of them opened in the fifth round), and US 2017/0318053 A1, the published application of US 9,853,999 B2 (#347). 33 were not: 15 were opened (entries below) and 18 were judged related (T1) on the title.
  - **169 do not.** 6 were in the data already. 5 were opened, power-sector or defence-allocation neighbours whose titles the rule could not judge (CN 117240502 A, related (T1); CN 120415882 A, excluded after reading; CN 120455078 A, excluded after reading; CN 120498762 A, excluded after reading; CN 120896776 B, related (T1)). The other 158 were counted and not entered (amendment 7).
- **What the figures show:** the keyword runs already held 27 of the 60 deception-titled patents that the tables name (45%), so they had not covered the neighbourhood of the closest patents. That measures the runs' reach around those patents, not recall in general: the tables are Google's selection, not a random sample.
- **Outcome:** 71 patents in the data carry P6: 38 found by this route alone (20 opened, 18 judged on the title) and 33 that earlier runs had found too. T2 0, related (T1) 68, excluded 3; pages opened 39. **None is T3 or T2**; D36 states the effect on A28 (`01-search-screening-record.md` §14–§17).
- **Limits, named:** the depth is two, fixed by Claude beforehand, and the route was not run to saturation. The tables of the other formal placement patents read at level 1 (CN 120658528 A, CN 117061224 A, CN 119383021 B, CN 118802292 B, CN 116155542 B, CN 115580430 A, CN 118400169 B) were not taken. A table shows what Google chooses to show. Patent rows with no deception term in the title were counted and not entered, so a placement method behind a neutral title that a table names is not in the data.

- **Raw pages:** read through the fetch tool, not saved.

641. **A29 run P6 — the papers named in the tables (15 November 2026).** The tables of the 16 pages name 193 non-patent documents. (2025), "Secure multiplicative sampled-data control for TS fuzzy-based cyber-physical systems subject to deception attacks" [CN 117240502 A], uses "deception attacks" in the control-theory sense of false-data attacks, the clash that block D leaves the word out for, and is excluded; its title also carries "cyber-physical", so §9 asks the user to confirm the exclusion (Claude recommends it stand). The other 35 were checked against this log by exact title and by author and year.
